## Spells

#### Aura of Evasion
*Level 7 Abjuration*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
An aura of alacrity radiates from you in a 30-foot Emanation for the duration. While in the aura, you and your allies have Advantage on Dexterity saving throws. When an affected creature is subjected to an effect that allows it to make a Dexterity saving throw to take only half damage, it takes no damage if it succeeds on the save and only half damage if it fails. A creature with the Incapacitated condition doesn't gain any benefit from being in the aura.

**Classes:** Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Battle Familiar
*Level 2 Conjuration*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 10 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (a diamond worth 25+ GP)
- **Duration:** 1 hour
---
You conjure a familiar imbued with magical might. The familiar appears in an unoccupied space within range; resembles an animal of your choice but is a Celestial, Fey, or Fiend (your choice); and uses the **Battle Familiar** stat block. When you cast this spell, choose the Brute, Flyer, or Stalker form. The choice determines certain details in the stat block.

***Combat.*** The familiar is an ally to you and your allies. It rolls its own Initiative and acts on its own turn.

***Disappearance of the Familiar.*** The battle familiar disappears when the spell ends, if it drops to 0 Hit Points, or if you die. When it disappears, it leaves behind in its space anything it was wearing or carrying. If you cast this spell again, you decide whether you summon the familiar that disappeared or a different one.

***One Familiar at a Time.*** You can't have more than one familiar at a time. If you cast this spell while you already have a familiar from this spell, that familiar is replaced by the new one.

If you have a familiar from the *Find Familiar* spell when you cast this spell, you empower your existing familiar rather than conjuring a new one. For the duration, your familiar can attack in addition to taking other actions as normal, and your familiar's game statistics are replaced by the Battle Familiar stat block, but it retains its creature type, alignment, Hit Points, Hit Point Dice, and any features granted by the *Find Familiar* spell. Your familiar gains a number of Temporary Hit Points equal to the Hit Points of the battle familiar. Battle Familiar ends early if the familiar has no more Temporary Hit Points left, and these Temporary Hit Points vanish if any remain when Battle Familiar ends. Your familiar reverts to its previous form when Battle Familiar ends.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** Use the spell slot's level for the spell's level in the stat block.

**Classes:** Druid, Warlock, Wizard

#### Catnap
*Level 3 Enchantment*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 30 feet
- **Components:** S, M (a pinch of sand)
- **Duration:** 10 minutes
---
You make a calming gesture, and up to three willing creatures of your choice that you can see within range have the Unconscious condition for the spell's duration. The spell ends on a target if it takes damage or another creature takes an action to shake it awake. If a target has the Unconscious condition from this spell for the full duration, that target gains the benefits of finishing a Short Rest, and it can't be affected by this spell again until it finishes a Long Rest.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** You can target one additional willing creature for each spell slot level above 3.

**Classes:** Artificer, Bard, Sorcerer, Wizard

#### Detonate
*Level 9 Evocation*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 500 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (a piece of tinder)
- **Duration:** Instantaneous
---
You create a magical, explosive seed inside a creature you can see within range. The target makes a Constitution saving throw, taking 10d10 Fire damage on a failed save or half as much damage on a successful one.

An explosion then originates from the target. Each creature in a 60-foot Emanation originating from the target (not including the target) makes a Dexterity saving throw, with Disadvantage if this spell reduced the target to 0 Hit Points. A creature takes 10d10 Fire damage on a failed save or half as much damage on a successful one.

A nonmagical object that isn't being worn or carried also takes the damage if it's in the Emanation, and the object starts burning if it's flammable.

**Classes:** Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Disruptive Tune
*Level 2 Abjuration*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
A distracting melody momentarily fills a 20-foot-radius Sphere centered on a point you choose within range. Each creature in the Sphere makes a Constitution saving throw. On a failed save, a target loses Concentration, and it has Disadvantage on Constitution saving throws to maintain Concentration for the spell's duration.

**Classes:** Bard, Sorcerer, Wizard

#### Distorted Distance
*Level 4 Illusion*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 10 minutes
---
You create an illusory spatial dilation that fills a 60-foot-radius Sphere centered on a point within range. For each creature you can see in the Sphere, you can choose one of the following effects for it:

- **Dizzying Elongation.** The creature makes an Intelligence saving throw. On a failed save, the creature takes 2d10 Psychic damage, and the Sphere is Difficult Terrain for it until the end of its turn.
- **Shortened Space.** The creature's Speed increases by 20 feet until the end of its next turn.
  Until the spell ends, whenever a creature enters the Sphere or ends its turn there, you can choose for it to receive one of these effects. A creature can be affected by this spell only once per turn.

**Classes:** Artificer, Bard, Warlock, Wizard

#### Dueling Ground
*Level 2 Abjuration*
___
- **Casting Time:** 10 minutes or Ritual
- **Range:** Touch
- **Components:** V, S, M (a silk flag worth 100+ GP)
- **Duration:** 1 hour
---
You create a magical dueling ground bounded by a glowing, rune-scribed circle in a 15-foot-radius Sphere centered on a point on the ground you touch.

As part of creating the dueling ground, you designate two willing creatures within the Sphere as the targets. The spell ends early if a creature other than one of the targets enters the Sphere.

If a target drops to 0 Hit Points while in the dueling ground, the target is Stable and teleports to the nearest unoccupied space outside the dueling ground. If a target is subjected to an effect that would kill it instantly without dealing damage while in the dueling ground, the target doesn't die and instead has 0 Hit Points and the Unconscious condition, is Stable, and teleports to the nearest unoccupied space outside the dueling ground.

When only one target remains in the dueling ground, runes matching those on the ground around the Sphere briefly flash around the remaining target's head like a crown.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** You can target one additional willing creature for each spell slot level above 2.

**Classes:** Artificer, Bard, Cleric, Druid, Paladin, Ranger, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Enervation
*Level 5 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 60 feet
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
A tendril of inky darkness reaches out from you to drain life from a creature you can see within range.

The target makes a Dexterity saving throw. On a failed save, the target takes 6d8 Necrotic damage. On each of your subsequent turns, you can take a Bonus Action to deal 2d8 Necrotic damage to the target automatically. The spell ends if the target is ever outside the spell's range or it has Total Cover from you.

On a successful save, the target takes half the initial damage only, and the spell ends.

Whenever this spell deals damage to a target, you regain Hit Points equal to half the amount of Necrotic damage dealt.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** The initial damage increases by 1d8 for each spell slot level above 5.

**Classes:** Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Entrancing Mirrors
*Level 8 Illusion*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 90 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (a mirror shard)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
You create dozens of illusory mirrors to confuse up to three creatures of your choice that you can see within range.

Each target makes an Intelligence saving throw. On a failed save, a target takes 7d6 Psychic damage and has the Stunned condition. While the target is Stunned, its Speed is halved. On a successful save, a target takes half as much damage only.

A Stunned target repeats the Intelligence saving throw at the end of each of its turns, ending the spell on itself on a success.

**Classes:** Bard, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Festering Blast
*Level 4 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** 1 minute
---
A 60-foot-long, 10-foot-wide Line of miasma blasts from you in a direction you choose. Each creature in the Line must make a Constitution saving throw. On a failed save, a target takes 4d10 Necrotic damage and has the Poisoned condition for the duration.

A creature Poisoned by this spell takes 2d10 Poison damage at the start of each of its turns. At the end of each of its turns, a Poisoned target repeats the save, ending the spell on itself on a success.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** The initial damage increases by 1d10 for each spell slot level above 4.

**Classes:** Druid, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Fractured Awareness
*Level 7 Divination*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
You cause a creature to receive conflicting visions of multiple possible futures. Choose a creature you can see within range. The target makes an Intelligence saving throw. On a failed save, the target takes 12d10 Psychic damage and has Disadvantage on D20 Tests for the duration. On a successful save, the target takes half as much damage only, and the spell ends.

At the end of each of its turns, an affected target repeats the save, ending the spell on a success.

**Classes:** Bard, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Grave Ground
*Level 5 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (a handful of grave dirt)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
Skeletal hands burst from an area on the ground within range. The area consists of up to four 10-foot squares, which you arrange as you like. Each square must be contiguous with at least one other square. That area is Difficult Terrain for your enemies.

Any enemy in that area when the skeletal hands appear makes a Strength saving throw. On a failed save, a creature takes 6d6 Necrotic damage, and until the end of its next turn, the creature subtracts 1d6 from all its damage rolls. An enemy also makes this save if it enters the area or ends its turn there. A creature makes this save only once per turn.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** The damage increases by 1d6 for each spell slot level above 5.

**Classes:** Cleric, Warlock, Wizard

#### Hindsight
*Level 9 Divination*
___
- **Casting Time:** 10 minutes
- **Range:** 500 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (a tiny hourglass worth 500+ GP, which the spell consumes)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 hour
---
You peer backward through the fabric of time. You see visions of events that occurred within range throughout the past 10 years, racing by at approximately 1 day per second. Throughout the spell's duration, you can freely slow down, pause, rewind, or fast-forward through these visions.

**Classes:** Wizard

#### Illusory Dragon
*Level 8 Illusion*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
By gathering threads of shadow material from the Shadowfell, you create a Huge shadowy dragon in an unoccupied space that you can see within range. The illusion lasts for the spell's duration and occupies its space as if it were a creature. The illusion is tangible because of the shadow material used to create it but has Immunity to all damage and conditions.

When the illusion appears, any enemy that can see it makes a Wisdom saving throw. On a failed save, the creature drops whatever it's holding and has the Frightened condition for the duration. If a creature Frightened in this way ends its turn in a space where it doesn't have line of sight to the illusion, the creature repeats the saving throw, ending the condition on itself on a success.

As a Bonus Action, you can move the illusion up to 60 feet. At any point during its movement, you can cause the shadowy dragon to exhale a blast of energy in a 60-foot Cone. Each creature in the Cone makes an Intelligence saving throw, taking 6d6 Acid, Cold, Fire, Lightning, Necrotic, or Poison damage (your choice when you create the dragon) on a failed save or half as much damage on a successful one.

A creature that takes a Study action to examine the dragon can determine that it is an illusion with a successful Intelligence (Investigation) check against your spell save DC. If a creature discerns the illusion for what it is, the creature can see through the image and has Advantage on saving throws to end the Frightened condition it caused and to resist its exhalation.

**Classes:** Wizard

#### Inflict Doubt
*Level 3 Enchantment*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
You inflict self-doubt on a creature you can see within range. The target must succeed on a Wisdom saving throw or have Disadvantage on D20 Tests for the duration. At the end of each of its turns, the target repeats the save, ending the spell on itself on a success.

**Classes:** Bard, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Invulnerability
*Level 9 Abjuration*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S, M (a piece of adamantine worth 500+ GP, which the spell consumes)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 10 minutes
---
You have Immunity to all damage until the spell ends.

**Classes:** Wizard

#### Iron Body
*Level 8 Transmutation*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Touch
- **Components:** V, S, M (diamond dust worth 250+ GP, which the spell consumes)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 hour
---
One willing creature you touch transforms into living metal. Until the spell ends, the target's Exhaustion level can't increase; the target has Resistance to Bludgeoning, Fire, Piercing, and Slashing damage; and it has Immunity to Poison damage and the Paralyzed, Petrified, and Poisoned conditions. If the target is Paralyzed, Petrified, or Poisoned when you cast the spell, those conditions immediately end for the target.

**Classes:** Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Lightning Ring
*Level 8 Evocation*
___
- **Casting Time:** Bonus action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S, M (a bit of fur and a glass ring)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 10 minutes
---
A ring of crackling electricity fills a 10-foot Emanation originating from you. Whenever the Emanation enters a creature's space and whenever a creature enters the Emanation or ends its turn there, you can force that creature to make a Constitution saving throw. On a failed save, the creature takes 3d6 Lightning damage plus 3d6 Thunder damage, and it has the Deafened condition for 1 minute. On a successful save, the creature takes half as much damage only.

As a Magic action while the spell is active, you can emit a 60-foot-long, 5-foot-wide Line out from you in a direction you choose. Each creature in the Line makes a Dexterity saving throw, taking 6d6 Lightning damage on a failed save or half as much damage on a successful one.

**Classes:** Druid, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Moment of Prescience
*Level 8 Divination*
___
- **Casting Time:** Reaction, which you take when you fail a D20 Test or when a creature hits you with an attack roll
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Instantaneous
---
You have a powerful sixth sense that guides you at just the right time. Turn the roll of your failed D20 Test into a 20, or turn the roll of the triggering attack roll into a 1.

**Classes:** Wizard

#### Mordenkainen's Lucubration
*Level 5 Divination*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Instantaneous
---
You recover up to two expended spell slots of level 2 or lower.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** The maximum level of spell slots you can recover increases to level 3 (if cast with a level 6-7 spell slot) or level 4 (if cast with a level 8+ spell slot).

**Classes:** Wizard

#### Negative Energy Flood
*Level 5 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 60 feet
- **Components:** V, M (a broken bone and a square of black silk)
- **Duration:** Instantaneous
---
You send ribbons of negative energy at one creature you can see within range. If the target isn't Undead, it makes a Constitution saving throw, taking 3d10 + 25 Necrotic damage on a failed save or half as much damage on a successful one. A Humanoid killed by this spell rises at the start of your next turn as a **Zombie** that follows your verbal orders for 24 hours, after which it acts according to its nature.

If the target is Undead, it gains 3d10 Temporary Hit Points.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** The damage increases by 1d10 for each spell slot level above 5.

**Classes:** Warlock, Wizard

#### Power Word Pain
*Level 7 Enchantment*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 60 feet
- **Components:** V
- **Duration:** 1 minute
---
You speak a word of power that causes waves of intense pain to assail one creature you can see within range. If the target has 100 Hit Points or fewer, it takes 6d8 Force damage and has the Charmed condition for the duration. Otherwise, it takes 6d8 Force damage only.

While the target is Charmed, its Speed can be no more than 10 feet, and it has Disadvantage on D20 Tests except Constitution saving throws. In addition, when the target tries to cast a spell, it must first succeed on a Constitution saving throw, or the spell dissipates with no effect, and the action, Bonus Action, or Reaction used to cast it is wasted. If that spell was cast with a spell slot, the slot isn't expended.

At the end of each of its turns, the Charmed target makes a Constitution saving throw, ending the spell on itself on a successful save.

**Classes:** Bard, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Reweave Fate
*Level 7 Divination*
___
- **Casting Time:** Reaction, which you take when a creature you can see within 60 feet of you fails a D20 Test
- **Range:** 60 feet
- **Components:** S
- **Duration:** Instantaneous
---
You untangle a single thread of fate to encourage a different result. The creature that failed the D20 Test can reroll it with Advantage, and the creature must use the new roll. If the D20 Test is successful, the creature gains 6d10 Temporary Hit Points from its strengthened fate.

**Classes:** Bard, Cleric, Warlock, Wizard

#### Spirit Lantern
*Level 5 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S, M (a black lantern)
- **Duration:** 10 minutes
---
You conjure a floating, ghostly black lantern that hovers above you and sheds Dim Light in a 60-foot radius. When an enemy dies within this Dim Light, a fragment of the creature's soul flies into the lantern. The lantern can hold a number of soul fragments equal to your spellcasting ability modifier, and those fragments dissipate when the spell ends.

As a Bonus Action, you can expend a soul fragment to create one of the following effects.

***Drain Life.*** The soul fragment darts at a creature of your choice that you can see within 60 feet of yourself. The target makes a Constitution saving throw, taking Necrotic damage equal to 4d8 plus your spellcasting ability modifier on a failed save or half as much damage on a successful one.

***Repair Undead.*** The soul fragment flies into an Undead creature of your choice that you can see within 60 feet of yourself. That creature regains Hit Points equal to 4d8 plus your spellcasting ability modifier.

***Ward Ally.*** The soul fragment darts around a creature of your choice that you can see within 60 feet of yourself. Other creatures have Disadvantage on attack rolls against the chosen creature until the start of your next turn.

**Classes:** Artificer, Cleric, Warlock, Wizard

#### Summon Dinosaur
*Level 6 Conjuration*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 90 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (polished scale worth 600+ GP)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 hour
---
You call forth the spirit of a primeval dinosaur. It manifests in an unoccupied space that you can see within range and uses the **Dinosaur Spirit** stat block. When you cast the spell, choose Ankylosaur, Triceratops, or Tyrannosaur. The creature resembles a dinosaur of the chosen type, which determines certain details in its stat block. The creature disappears when it drops to 0 Hit Points or when the spell ends.

The creature is an ally to you and your allies. In combat, the creature shares your Initiative count, but it takes its turn immediately after yours. It obeys your verbal commands (no action required by you). If you don't issue any, the creature takes the Dodge action and uses its movement to avoid danger.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** Use the spell slot's level for the spell's level in the stat block.

**Classes:** Druid

#### Summon Plant
*Level 5 Conjuration*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 90 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (herbs worth 500+ GP)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 hour
---
You call forth the spirit of an animated plant. It manifests in an unoccupied space that you can see within range and uses the **Plant Spirit** stat block. When you cast the spell, choose Fungus, Tree, or Vine. The creature resembles a plant of the chosen type, which determines certain details in its stat block. The creature disappears when it drops to 0 Hit Points or when the spell ends.

The creature is an ally to you and your allies. In combat, the creature shares your Initiative count, but it takes its turn immediately after yours. It obeys your verbal commands (no action required by you). If you don't issue any, it takes the Dodge action and uses its movement to avoid danger.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** Use the spell slot's level for the spell's level in the stat block.

**Classes:** Druid, Ranger

#### Transfix
*Level 7 Enchantment*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
For the duration, your appearance becomes otherworldly and alluring. One creature of your choice that you can see within 60 feet of you must succeed on a Charisma saving throw or have the Charmed condition for the duration.

While Charmed, the target has the Incapacitated condition, and if the target is more than 5 feet away from you, it moves on its turn toward you by the most direct route, trying to get within 5 feet of you. If the Charmed creature ends its turn within 5 feet of you, it takes 4d8 Psychic damage.

The target doesn't avoid Opportunity Attacks when moving toward you, but before moving into damaging terrain (such as lava or a pit), the target repeats the save, ending the spell on itself on a success.

Until the spell ends, you can take a Magic action on subsequent turns to target another creature, but you can't target a creature again if it has succeeded on a save against this casting of the spell.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** The damage increases by 1d8 for each spell slot level above 7.

**Classes:** Bard, Warlock, Wizard

#### Uncertain Footing
*Level 2 Illusion*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (a distorted lens)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
You create illusory obstacles such as rocks or spikes in an attempt to confuse up to three creatures you can see within range, affecting their ability to move. Each target makes an Intelligence saving throw. On a failed save, a target's Speed is halved, and it can't take the Dash action. A hampered target repeats the save at the end of each of its turns, ending the spell on itself on a success.

**Classes:** Artificer, Bard, Warlock, Wizard

#### Vision of Elapsing Eons
*Level 9 Illusion*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 120 feet
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** 1 minute
---
You trick a creature you can see within range into believing it is watching itself and its surroundings crumble away, as if eons were passing in moments. The target must succeed on an Intelligence saving throw or take 10d12 Psychic damage and have the Paralyzed condition for the duration.

While Paralyzed, the target repeats the save at the end of each of its turns. On a failed save, it gains 1 Exhaustion level. On a successful save, the spell ends. The spell ends early if someone within 5 feet of the target takes an action to shake it free from the illusion.

**Classes:** Bard, Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Wail of the Banshee
*Level 9 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 60 feet
- **Components:** V
- **Duration:** Instantaneous
---
You emit a terrible scream that can kill those who hear it. Choose up to ten creatures within range. Each target with 50 Hit Points or fewer dies. Targets with more than 50 Hit Points make a Constitution saving throw. On a failed save, a target takes 12d10 Psychic damage and has the Deafened condition for 1 hour. On a successful save, a target takes half as much damage only. A target is unaffected by the spell if it can't hear you.

**Classes:** Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Waves of Exhaustion
*Level 5 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Bonus action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S, M (a piece of dried meat)
- **Duration:** Concentration, up to 1 minute
---
You evoke a nimbus of flickering gray light around your body. For the duration, you can take a Magic action to emit a wave of gray light in a 60-foot Cone. Creatures in that area must succeed on a Constitution saving throw or gain 1 Exhaustion level. This spell can't increase a creature's Exhaustion level above 4.

Exhaustion levels gained from this spell are removed when the spell ends.

**Classes:** Sorcerer, Warlock, Wizard

#### Wither and Bloom
*Level 2 Necromancy*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** 60 feet
- **Components:** V, S, M (a withered vine twisted into a loop)
- **Duration:** Instantaneous
---
You invoke both death and life in a 10-foot-radius Sphere centered on a point within range. Each creature of your choice in that area makes a Constitution saving throw, taking 3d6 Necrotic damage on a failed save or half as much damage on a successful one. Nonmagical plants in that area that aren't creatures, such as trees and shrubs, wither.

In addition, one creature of your choice in that area can roll one of its unexpended Hit Point Dice and regain a number of Hit Points equal to the roll plus your spellcasting ability modifier. That die is then expended.
***Using a Higher-Level Spell Slot.*** The damage increases by 1d6 for each spell slot level above 2. The number of Hit Point Dice that can be rolled and expended increases by one for each spell slot level above 2. You add your spellcasting modifier to the number of Hit Points regained by this spell only once, regardless of the number of Hit Point Dice rolled and expended.

**Classes:** Druid, Sorcerer, Wizard

#### Zone of Amicability
*Level 4 Enchantment*
___
- **Casting Time:** Action
- **Range:** Self
- **Components:** V, S
- **Duration:** 10 minutes
---
A magical zone of amicability radiates from you in a 60-foot Emanation for the duration. When you make an ability check to influence a creature in that area, you can treat a d20 roll of 9 or lower as a 10.

**Classes:** Bard, Warlock

# Chapter 3: Creating Magical Worlds

![In highly magical worlds, people use magic for major tasks such as construction.](img/book/AU/028-03-001.ch-3-splash.webp)

Using the advice and tools in this chapter, you can create your own world infused with magic for your D&D games. This chapter also provides guidelines for tailoring a group patron for your characters' adventures, helping the players feel immersed in your setting. When creating your magical world, you can choose the options you like best or modify an existing world using your favorite advice. For example, you could use an archmage group patron of your own creation in your adventures that take place in the Forgotten Realms.

## Magic in Existing Worlds

If you want to use this chapter's advice within an existing world, consider the ones on the D&D Settings table. It describes the magic prevalent in each setting as well as where you can find more information about those settings.

## Building a Magical World

The advice presented here will help you build your own magical world. Each of the sections that follow focuses on a different aspect of world-building, beginning with the broadest concepts and proceeding to specific topics, including questions designed to guide your creativity. Examples from throughout the D&D multiverse illustrate approaches to each of these subjects:

##### D&D Settings
| Setting          | Description                                                                                                                                       | Sourcebook                                                                        |
|------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Dragonlance      | Draconic magic is a major theme in this setting consumed by war. The use of magic granted by gods is rare.                                        | *Dragonlance: Shadow of the Dragon Queen*                                         |
| Eberron          | Magic is ubiquitous and functions like technology, providing services such as rapid transportation and long-distance communication.               | *Eberron: Forge of the Artificer* and *Eberron: Rising from the Last War*         |
| Forgotten Realms | Many types of magic infuse dozens of realms. The gods walk in disguise among mortals.                                                             | *Forgotten Realms: Heroes of Faerûn* and *Forgotten Realms: Adventures in Faerûn* |
| Greyhawk         | Magic is widespread and commonly used but still wondrous and sometimes frightening.                                                               | *Dungeon Master's Guide*                                                          |
| Ravenloft        | Magical mists ensure each domain is a prison for its magical Darklord, or ruler, and the hapless residents here. Magic here is ominous and scary. | *Ravenloft: The Horrors Within* and *Curse of Strahd*                             |

- Outline Your World. Which types of magic and realms exist in your world?
- Attitudes Toward Magic. How do people in your realms view magic and its use?
- Religion. What gods of magic exist?
- Technology. Does technology also exist, and if so, how do magic and technology interact?
- Laws. Do laws regulate or encourage the use of magic?
- Commerce. How does the business of spellcasting and magic items function, and are mages' guilds prominent?
- Magical Armies. How do the use and prevalence of magic affect warfare and the forces used to fight it?
- Mastering Magic. How do mages learn magic, and what forms do magical academies take?

>>*Magical worlds beyond the dreams of the most learned scholars hold limitless knowledge. Mystra willing, you might master their gifts. Use this power wisely, friends.*— Alustriel

### Outline Your World

![Performers infuse their music with magic to create a joyous environment.](img/book/AU/029-03-002.magic-performers.webp)

Before you create individual realms and societies, determine basic details about magic in your world. Consider which categories of magic are most or least prevalent, how that magic shaped the world's geography, and the nature of the major realms.

#### Categories of Magic

In the worlds of D&D, magic can be categorized in three main ways: by different approaches (arcane or divine), by the schools of magic, and by distinct magical traditions. You might use any of these categories to define the role of magic in your world.

***Approaches to Magic.*** The spells of Bards, Sorcerers, Warlocks, and Wizards are commonly called arcane magic. These practitioners rely on direct apprehension of the laws and principles of magic. Wizards study those principles directly, Sorcerers and Bards have a more intuitive understanding, and Warlocks gain that knowledge through their pacts.

The spells of Clerics, Druids, Paladins, and Rangers are called divine magic, though the nature magic of Druids and Rangers is sometimes divided into its own category: primal magic. All these practitioners approach magic mediated by gods, the forces of nature, the sacred weight of a Paladin's oath, or some other divine force.

***Schools of Magic.*** The eight schools of magic are Abjuration, Conjuration, Divination, Enchantment, Evocation, Illusion, Necromancy, and Transmutation. These schools represent the underpinnings of all magic in a D&D world. All D&D spells belong to one of these schools.

In your world, spellcasters might experience sensory effects when casting spells of different schools. For example, their scalps might tingle while casting Evocation spells, they might see flashes of events in their peripheral vision while casting Divination spells, or they might smell the phantom scent of grave soil while casting Necromancy spells.

***Magical Traditions.*** Spellcasters of any sort might practice a wide variety of magical traditions. Dragons claim to be the originators of magic on some worlds. Some magical traditions rely entirely on rituals (which characters can access through the Ritual Caster feat in the *Player's Handbook*). Rune magic (discussed in *Bigby Presents: Glory of the Giants*) was pioneered by giants and represents a distinct magical tradition. In the Forgotten Realms setting, ancient elves developed the tradition of high magic and used it to transform the world. Spellcasters in that setting also developed the practice of Circle magic (detailed in *Forgotten Realms: Heroes of Faerûn*), which allows spellcasters to pool their efforts to cast powerful spells. Magical traditions can also be passed down through family lines, as is the case with Eberron's dragonmarks—magical birthmarks that grant the use of specific spells (as described in *Eberron: Forge of the Artificer*).

#### Magical Geography

Think about the ways magic has shaped the physical landscape of your world. Perhaps a powerful archmage, desiring privacy, used magic to raise mountains around her home. Or a nation governed by powerful mages might cloak the realm in protective magic to block its enemies' magical prying. Your world might boast enormous monuments or strange geographical features created by magic in eons long gone or left behind by the gods in an ancient war.

Magic can have more modest effects too, which nonetheless impact the daily life of your world's residents. For example, in Calimport in the Forgotten Realms, a magical lake inside the city supplies water for residents, while a floating tower serves as headquarters for soldiers who ride flying carpets. The more common magic is in your setting, the more visible its effects should be throughout the world.

#### Magical Realms

As you think about your world's geography, consider the realms within it. Are they all home to powerful spellcasters? Are some more reliant on magic than others?

Your world might include three realms that each rely on a different type of magic. For example, one realm is ruled by wizards who each focus on a particular school of magic. In contrast, another realm is the domain of warlocks who gain their powers from a mysterious, otherworldly entity, while the third realm is ruled by sorcerers who descend from dragons and those who worshipped them. This emphasis on different kinds of spellcasting in each realm gives each a distinct flavor and culture.

On the other hand, you might find it useful to have realms in your world that don't emphasize magic to serve as a contrast to the realms that do. A small realm surrounded by warlike neighbors with large armies of ordinary soldiers, for example, might turn to magic to defend itself and become a haven for mages.

### Attitudes Toward Magic

Once you've determined the types of magic that pervade your world, consider how your world's inhabitants view magic. Do they welcome magic and the potential it represents? Or do they resent and fear magic, seeing it as a force for unexpected change and danger? Do attitudes vary by region?

#### Trust in or Distrust of Magic

A society that has relied on magic for generations has likely grown accustomed to magic's effects on daily life. Such societies' traditions and practices take magic into account. If access to the *Find Familiar* spell is common, for example, public spaces and businesses would be designed to accommodate familiars as well as people.

Alternatively, a society might distrust magic that's not a part of daily life. Perhaps an unfamiliar kind of magic is blamed for a memorable tragedy, leading people to distrust that type of magic. For example, a realm that's been invaded by an army of undead might prohibit necromancy even if the realm encourages other schools of magic.

#### Magical Shortcuts

Magic can often accomplish tasks more quickly and easily than is normally possible. While people in a magic-rich society easily accept the convenience and reliability of magical substitutes for common services, there might be a backlash effect or unusual side effects from folk taking magic for granted.

For example, wealthy nobles might seek out a chef capable of making food "the old-fashioned way." Or a society rife with healing magic might become obsessed with thrill-seeking and dangerous sports.

### Religion

Most D&D worlds have many gods, and a magical society usually has at least one god of magic.

Are there gods of magic in your world? Who are they? What do they expect of their worshippers and from individuals who wield magic? Are they benevolent, antagonistic, or ambivalent toward mortals?

#### Benevolent Magic Gods

You might include a benevolent god of magic in your world. Such a god believes magic should be used for good, improving the lives of ordinary people. They encourage teaching and promoting the use of magic, and aspiring mages are expected to maintain a moral and ethical code that emphasizes selflessness, duty, and charity.

For example, crafters in a world with a benevolent god of magic are encouraged to make Common magic items that benefit others. When adventurers recover lost magic from ancient dungeons, priests hide dangerous magic away while they share useful magic with as many people as possible.

Including a benevolent god of magic in your world doesn't preclude the inclusion of evil mages. Indeed, a benevolent god of magic might be outnumbered or outmatched by evil rivals. This prompts the god of magic or their followers to seek help from the characters.

***Mystra.*** Mystra is a benevolent god of magic in the Forgotten Realms setting. Mystra has been destroyed or defeated multiple times, but each time she has returned in a new form with the help of brave adventurers.

#### Antagonistic Magic Gods

A god of magic might spread evil, cruelty, and tyranny through the use of magic. An antagonistic god of magic favors nefarious uses of magic. Such a god's necromancer followers might raise large armies of undead to unleash on the population. Or adherents might use powerful Evocation spells to destroy rival cities and those who live in them. An antagonistic god of magic might rule a realm dedicated to evil and populated with undead, devils or demons, and other bloodthirsty creatures.

***Iuz and Vecna.*** The demigod Iuz, described in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*, and *Vecna*, whose schemes to amass ultimate power are described in *Vecna: Eve of Ruin*, are both antagonistic gods who seek conquest through magic. Iuz is primarily a god of deceit and oppression, but his methods involve seeking ancient magic he can use to increase his power. Vecna plots from the shadows and reveals himself only when victory is nigh.

#### Ambivalent Magic Gods

A god of magic might be ambivalent about their followers. Such a deity cares more about whether magic is used than about how it is used. When characters recover lost magical secrets during an adventure, an ambivalent god of magic might want those magical secrets locked away and preserved—not out of a selfish desire to control the information, but out of an intellectual interest in ensuring this knowledge isn't lost again.

***Mystryl.*** An ambivalent god of magic might guard the source of all magic or even embody magic. Mystryl, the original god of magic in the Forgotten Realms, embodied magic in this way. When Mystryl died, magic was transformed; spellcasting became unreliable, and spellcasters lost their ability to cast their most powerful spells. Magic stabilized only when the god of magic returned as Mystra.

***Boccob.*** An ambivalent god of magic in the Greyhawk setting and patron of the legendary mage *Mordenkainen*, Boccob works to ensure that good and evil forces remain equal.

#### Weaving a Divine Tapestry

A high-magic setting might include multiple gods of magic, each with conflicting goals and methods. No single god is likely to completely satisfy a society ruled by mages where spellcasting is common. Including multiple competing gods of magic gives your players more choices for patron gods for their characters. The conflict between rival gods of magic can create adventures and drive campaigns.

Your world might have different gods for each school of magic (a god of Conjuration, for example, and a god of Transmutation). Or a primary god of magic might be assisted by other gods, each with their own emphasis. For example, Mystra is assisted by Azuth, a god of wizardry who is ambivalent in his outlook and uninterested in the moral and ethical use of magic.

Your world might even have no god focused entirely on magic. In a world where magic suffuses every aspect of life, every god might carry some responsibility for the use of magic as it impacts the rest of their portfolio. A god of war cares not just about weapons and bloodshed, but about the use of magic in battle. A god of crafting might have dominion over making magic items and over artisans who use magic in their trades. And a god of knowledge might govern the academic study of magic often associated with gods of magic. Eberron's pantheon is a good example.

### Technology

Many D&D campaigns assume the technology of a medieval or early renaissance setting. However, the *Dungeon Master's Guide* has rules for modern firearms and futuristic weapons such as *Antimatter Rifles* and *Laser Pistols*. Further examples of futuristic technology can be found in *Quests from the Infinite Staircase*.

When you create your magical world, consider what level of technology is present. Does advanced technology exist in your setting? If it does, what do people think of it? Is technology a replacement for or a supplement to magic?

#### Technological Revolution

To introduce more advanced technology into your world, you can present it as originating in a recent technological revolution. An ancient and magic-focused realm ruled by mages might suddenly discover technological innovation. This could streamline daily life with simple technology, such as long-range communications, and add options for characters who like technology. It could also threaten the realm's rulers and therefore lead to technology being heavily regulated. Threatened rulers might hunt down inventors and destroy their creations. On the flip side, rulers might feel threatened by magic-users, leading these rulers to embrace technology to stave off power-hungry mages.

#### Rival Powers

![In Eberron, magic mimics technology, allowing wonders such as airships.](img/book/AU/030-03-003.eberron-magic.webp)

Alternatively, you can introduce technology to your world by creating a rival realm that relies on technology much like your magical society relies on magic. These two realms could be equivalent in wealth, status, and influence, but what one accomplishes with spells, the other achieves through machines.

Consider whether characters might come from either realm, or if the entire party hails from one society or the other. The first case encourages wildly different characters; some heroes enter battle with the *Magic Missile* and *Fireball* spells prepared, while others rely on their trusty *Laser Pistols* and *Grenade Launchers*.

If all characters come from one of the realms, the other society can serve as a rival and antagonist. If, for example, all your characters are from a magical society bordering a high-technology realm, tension between these two lands runs high and conflict is inevitable. Your campaign might begin with the characters infiltrating their technologically advanced neighbor while concealing their magical skills. There, the characters discover a plan for a mechanical army to invade the party's homeland. To thwart this invasion, the characters need to lead a force of conjured creatures, undead, and other magical warriors.

#### Magic and Technology in Tandem

Magic and technology need not be oppositional forces. You can create a society in which magic and technology operate side by side. In such a realm, bandits are just as likely to cast the *Fire Bolt* spell as they are to draw a *Laser Pistol*. Citizens predict the weather with the *Druidcraft* spell, but they tell time using a clock in the middle of town.

***Eberron.*** In a variant of this approach, magic provides the power for technology. Magic is used this way in Eberron. Eberron has airships, trains, and other examples of industrial technology, but these devices are powered by spells, magic items, and Elementals instead of nonmagical batteries and engines. Instantaneous, long-distance communication is possible in Eberron through *Sending Stones* staffed by members of a dragonmarked house; the result is akin to a magical telegraph network. Similarly, the residents of your society might have access to magic items that duplicate the effects of phones, automobiles, and similar modern conveniences.

### Laws

Even a novice spellcaster, able to cast nothing more than a cantrip, can use their magic to kill. Powerful mages can secretly observe individuals from afar, probe the thoughts of others, or control minds. It stands to reason that magical societies would impose laws to restrict the use of invasive, coercive, and destructive magic.

When creating your magical world, consider how the law restricts the use of magic, if at all. Are mages punished only when their magic hurts others? Are certain spells always restricted, or does anything go?

#### Outlawed Magic

The simplest magic law is the strictest: a ban on all magic. Laws like this come about because a society has a long history of conflict with magical enemies, because magic is blamed for environmental or social ills, or because of simple superstition. Power-hungry mages who run a society might outlaw the use of magic by anyone other than the ruling class.

***Vilhon Reach.*** Several regions of the Vilhon Reach in the Forgotten Realms curtail or ban magic, particularly magic used to charm or injure others.

#### Focusing on the Crime

Some magical societies consider laws against the use of magic unnecessary when existing laws already prohibit the harmful effects of magic. After all, murder is murder, whether the victim was killed with a sword, a spell, or the killer's bare hands.

For example, a large city like Greyhawk or Waterdeep might have laws against theft and laws that prohibit spying on a person in their home. An individual caught performing such acts would be guilty of a crime whether they cast the *Invisibility* spell or used nonmagical means.

Magic might count as an aggravating factor during sentencing, however. An individual found guilty of murder who killed the victim with the *Finger of Death* spell might be given a harsher sentence by a judge.

#### Frontier Justice

In remote regions that lack effective law enforcement, individuals and communities decide how magic and mages are treated. Residents might be suspicious of magic and intolerant of public spellcasting. Or they might become antagonistic toward mages who use magic in a way the residents don't like. As a third option, a single law enforcement official, such as a sheriff or marshal, might decide which mages are allowed to practice their craft undisturbed and which are driven out of town.

***Ten-Towns.*** In the remote settlements of Ten-Towns in the Forgotten Realms, an innkeeper might hang up a "No Spells Allowed" sign and hire a bouncer or two to enforce this rule. Putting up such a sign is the innkeeper's right, but the town guard (if there is a town guard) is under no obligation to enforce it.

### Commerce

How magic intertwines with commerce in your world is a key part of how your characters interact with it.

How easily can your characters find magic item shops or mages to purchase spellcasting services from? How accessible are magic items of higher rarity, such as Legendary magic items or Artifacts? Do mages' guilds regulate the accessibility of magic?

#### Magic Item Shops

![Members of House Deneith in Eberron work as paid, magic-wielding bodyguards.](img/book/AU/031-03-004.house-deneith.webp)

While *Spell Scrolls* and *Potions of Healing* are included in gear a character can buy in the *Player's Handbook*, the *Dungeon Master's Guide* leaves it up to the DM to decide what magic items are available to purchase in a campaign. A variety of magic item shops might be common in your world.

If you want Rare or rarer magic items to feel special, treat them like antiquities or fine art. A magic item shop in such a setting could be owned and operated by a single person who has deep knowledge of magical lore and contacts all over the world. When an adventurer asks for a specific magic item, the shop owner can procure it eventually, but the search takes time and leads to an adventure.

If magic items are more common in your world, a magic item shop in a city might look more like a pawnshop. All kinds of magic items would line the shelves, including duplicates of especially useful items such as *Bags of Holding*. The owner might be assisted by several individuals who specialize in security and deterring theft.

#### Magical Businesses

While any mage can sell their spellcasting services, your magical society might be filled with mages who use magic to earn a living in more interesting ways. The *Player's Handbook* presents availability and cost details for spellcasting services, but you can alter this availability as best suits your world.

For example, a novice spellcaster who can cast the *Prestidigitation* spell could operate a laundry service, quickly and easily cleaning clothes. A chef could use the same spell to flavor food for an hour. Cantrips such as *Elementalism*, *Guidance*, *Mending*, and *Spare the Dying* are also well suited for a humble but reliable magical career.

Magical businesses could take dramatic forms in your setting. Eberron is full of examples, from the magical artificers of House Cannith to the magical bodyguards of House Deneith.

#### Mages' Guilds

A mages' guild is an alliance of mages who run a robust magical business in a city. Such guilds are a staple of fantasy roleplaying games, especially campaigns with roots in D&D's earliest editions and settings, such as Greyhawk.

***Guild Practices.*** A successful mages' guild works hand in hand with the local law enforcement. Typically, local laws don't prohibit spellcasting or making magic items, but they prohibit those who don't belong to the guild from making a profit by providing services that fall under the guild's exclusive concerns.

The local law enforcement typically includes members who are mages—and members of the guild. Both the guild and law enforcement tend to spend considerable time and effort monitoring mages in the city, enforcing guild rules and looking for mages avoiding guild membership.

***Membership.*** Mages in a guild enjoy a monopoly on magical commerce. They expect to be paid an agreed-on amount of money for their services. Further, the guild is an excellent place for networking with other mages, and it probably operates a guildhall that includes a library of magical tomes and spellbooks as well as dining facilities, sleeping areas, and more. A guildhall could serve as an excellent base of operations for a group of characters connected to the guild.

Guild members are expected to pay dues and obey the guild's rules. In this way, a mages' guild in your world could serve as your characters' employer—as well as their debt holder.

***Rank.*** Membership in the guild might be structured into three or more ranks. While members of the lowest rank have no influence in the guild and are expected to assist senior members for little or no pay, high-ranking members enjoy a wealthy lifestyle, authority in the guild, and the assistance of lower-ranking members.

***Campaign Role.*** A mages' guild can serve as a group patron for characters or as an adversary that must be overcome. As a patron, the guild offers access to spellbooks, a comfortable refuge from danger, and magical allies knowledgeable on numerous subjects.

But a mages' guild might instead be an oppressive organization that ruthlessly exploits its monopoly and harasses nonmembers. Your campaign could focus on ousting the guild's leaders and replacing them with egalitarian leadership.

### Magical Armies

![A War Wizard of Cormyr casts a powerful Evocation spell on the battlefield.](img/book/AU/032-03-005.war-wizard-of-cormyr.webp)

Fantasy realms raise and maintain armies to defend their territories. A society steeped in magic might establish magical armies to complement its regular troops. What benefits do magical armies provide, and what forms do they take?

#### War Wizards

A magical army might boast mages in its ranks, either mixed in among ordinary soldiers or forming elite units composed entirely of spellcasters. The War Wizards of Cormyr in the Forgotten Realms is an archetypal example of this kind of elite unit.

War wizards defy mage stereotypes. They often wear armor and wield martial weapons, resembling Eldritch Knights. Alternatively, a war wizard might wear simple robes and wield a staff but focus on damaging spells. These mages function as mobile artillery units, protected by martial soldiers. **Spellguards** (see chapter 6) might feature prominently in such an army.

#### Magical Minions

A magical army might supplement or even replace ordinary soldiers with magical minions. During the Last War, Eberron's armies extensively used magical minions, with the nation of Karrnath fielding armies of zombies and skeletons while Cyre and other nations used constructs. Outside Eberron, other magical realms fill their armies with conjured creatures or elementals summoned from the Inner Planes.

Fielding magical minions allows commanders and martial soldiers to remain safely behind the battle line while the minions participate in combat. A realm heavily dependent on magical minions might enter war more quickly than other realms and take more risks on the battlefield, brushing off the loss of magical minions as insignificant since they're easily replaced.

#### Magical Fortifications

A traditional castle isn't much of a defense against mages who can cast spells such as *Fly*, *Passwall*, and *Teleport*. Fortunately, a realm with magical resources has many magical ways to defend itself. Defensive measures such as the *Wall of Force*, *Globe of Invulnerability*, and *Antimagic Field* spells cover a small area for a limited time and are useful in emergencies. But options such as the *Forbiddance* and *Guards and Wards* spells last much longer and can provide layers of magical defenses. Entrances can be protected with the *Glyph of Warding* spell.

A fortification might be protected by magical defenders that negate an enemy's advantages. For example, flying assailants could be countered with flying defenders, such as soldiers mounted on griffins, hippogriffs, or pegasi.

### Mastering Magic

![In your world, magical instruction might involve hundreds of students learning from masters in an arena such as this one.](img/book/AU/033-03-006.magical-arena.webp)

If magic suffuses your world, consider how its inhabitants learn their magical craft. Is magic laboriously taught by an elderly expert to a single apprentice? Do aspiring mages enroll at an academy where they're taught alongside others? Or do novice spellcasters learn another way?

The forms of magical instruction can be described in four ways: solitary study, apprenticeship, academic instruction, or cultural immersion, where magical instruction is readily available to all. Most magical societies rely on a single type of magical instruction. You might decide, however, that different kinds of instruction are prevalent in different parts of the world. For example, a magical realm might boast a magical academy with hundreds of students, but in the realm's capital, magic might be so common that everyone knows a spell or two thanks to the cultural prevalence of magic in everyday life.

#### Solitary Study

When magic is learned through solitary study, each mage understands magic slightly differently. For example, one mage might conceive of magic as influenced by the stars and planets, while another conceptualizes magic as an imperceptible force that connects all things. Both mages can cast spells of equivalent strength and effect, but their understanding of the underlying origin of magic varies.

***Warlocks.*** In a society that relies on solitary study, every mage is self-taught. Warlocks, who cobble together magical power from pacts and little-understood scraps of lore, are the most common kind of spellcaster in these societies. Wizards, who traditionally rely on learning from others, might be rare.

#### Apprenticeship

When magic is learned through apprenticeship, magical instruction is limited to one-on-one relationships between an expert and a novice. An experienced mage might have many apprentices in a lifetime but teach only one at a time. As a result, there are always more candidates for apprenticeship than mentors, and competition among potential apprentices might be intense. Experienced mages might screen candidates or develop elaborate tests for prospective apprentices, measuring their intelligence, loyalty, magical acumen, or ethical code. These tests might serve as low-level adventures in your world.

***Campaigns.*** In a campaign focused on apprenticeship, the mentor becomes unusually important. Craft interesting mentors your players will enjoy interacting with. The archetypal mentor is enigmatic and seemingly omnipotent, but they can also be whimsical, grouchy, or absent-minded.

In addition to creating a memorable mentor, consider when and how your characters' apprenticeships end. Is a level 1 Wizard finished with their apprenticeship? Or do they remain an apprentice until level 3 or later? How does the mentor decide when the student has learned enough?

***Sorcerers.*** Sorcerers flourish in societies focused on apprenticeship. Many sorcerers inherit their powers from a magical ancestor, or their powers might be shared among members of their family. These family connections make it easier to secure a mentor, and the family might have well-established traditions around recognizing, controlling, and mastering magic.

#### Academic Instruction

In a long-established magical society, the myriad magical philosophies taught by individual mentors eventually coalesce around the most successful teachers. When these teachers pool their resources to form a magical academy, large numbers of students can benefit from academic instruction. Styles of magical academies are further detailed in "Magical Academies" below.

***Wizards.*** Wizards flourish in a magical society with academic instruction. Dutifully showing up to school every day, books in hand, Wizards learn new spells in a classroom environment. During off hours, they practice magic, pursue magical interests on their own, and get into the sort of trouble students always find.

#### Cultural Immersion

Magic in your society might be so well understood and so widespread that aspiring mages can learn basic magic from everyday life. In such a society, books of magical instruction might be found at any bookstore or library. Everyone might know a spell or two, and learning a new one might be as easy as sitting down for a few hours with a friend and exchanging techniques. When magic is widely understood and a part of everyday life, you don't need to create magical academies or mentor figures to explain why characters know basic magic.

***Bards.*** Bards—whose magic takes the form of music, dance, and other popular art forms—flourish in a society where magic is popular. To Bards, learning new magic might be as simple as overhearing a catchy tune in a tavern and reworking it for their own purposes.

#### Magical Academies

The magical academies in the worlds of D&D make useful models for your world-building. Consider the following when creating your magical academy.

![Magical academies sometimes have their own sports, such as the game of Mage Tower at Strixhaven.](img/book/AU/034-03-007.magical-academies.webp)

***Everything Under One Roof.*** The most common form of magical academy is a single place where several kinds of magic are taught. For example, Strixhaven, detailed in *Strixhaven: A Curriculum of Chaos*, is a magical academy where five different campuses teach opposing magic philosophies. Similarly, the Tower of High Sorcery in the Dragonlance setting, described in *Dragonlance: Shadow of the Dragon Queen*, teaches that setting's three primary orders of magic.

An academy of this sort is home to mages with different philosophies, goals, and methods. These individuals are united around the higher goal of teaching magic to the next generation of students. A student at one of these academies has access to a wide variety of spells, magical traditions, and instructors. Because students come from far away, they benefit from interacting with people from all over the world.

This kind of academy provides players with a lot of character creation options. Characters of any background, species, class, or subclass might come together to study magic and are sent on adventures by teachers with competing agendas. A magical academy is an example of an esoteric institution group patron, detailed later in this chapter.

***Rival Academies.*** A realm with a large population of aspiring mages might have multiple magical academies that each specialize in a different form of magic.

An academy of this sort might focus on a single kind of magic: for instance, magic from the Conjuration school, or bladesinging, an ancient elven technique of magical combat. Rivalries between such academies take forms ranging from athletic competitions to pranks, clandestine raids, or even physical violence.

Because all students at one such academy study a single kind of magic, the academy is less likely to function as the home base for your campaign. Instead, the rival academies likely feature in the backstories of characters who wield magic, serving as the source of the characters' early magical training. However, you might create a campaign in which graduates of these rival academies are brought together by a menace that threatens all the academies or even the entire realm.

> ##### Magical Academies in the Real World
>
>In our world, old traditions speak of schools where eager students went to learn magic. Transylvanian legend describes the Scholomance, a school for sorcery where the devil taught ten students at a time. At the end of their term of instruction, nine students were allowed to depart, but the devil claimed the tenth. Other examples of magical academies in folklore include the Cave of Salamanca in Spain and Domdaniel, a magical school first mentioned in a French continuation of The Arabian Nights.
>

## Magical Group Patrons

Whether your heroes wish to advance the agenda of a famous mage or seek access to a magical academy's ample resources, a group patron with significant magical prowess can create the cohesion that holds a campaign together and help your game feel infused with magic.

This section presents six types of magical group patrons around which you can create campaigns, as summarized on the Magical Patron Options table.

Each sample patron includes examples from the D&D multiverse and outlines for patronage campaigns. You can incorporate these into your game or use them as inspiration for your own magical group patron.

This section also explores the option of your group's patron hiding their identity for reasons benevolent or malign, which can be used with any of the group patrons that follow.

##### Magical Patron Options
| Category             | Description                                                                                |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Archmage             | A powerful, often famous mage with clear goals that span the multiverse                    |
| Esoteric Institution | An organization dedicated to education, research, or containing dangerous magic            |
| Magical Entity       | A non-Humanoid mage or magical creature with a mysterious, timeless outlook                |
| Political Faction    | A group of powerful, like-minded mages deeply invested in political goals                  |
| Trickster            | A capricious individual with inscrutable motives and a malevolent or whimsical nature      |
| Wizards' Circle      | An alliance of mages focused on pursuing a difficult goal or resolving a problem or threat |

### How Patrons Work

No matter their details, group patrons provide adventurers with some common benefits.

#### Group Assistance

Having a group patron gives an adventuring group a common purpose, which inspires better coordination in the form of guidance and encouragement. As a result of this connection, each member of your party can grant Advantage on a D20 Test made by another member of the party. To grant Advantage in this way, you and your target must be able to see or hear each other, and neither can have the Incapacitated condition. After granting Advantage in this way, you can't do so again until you finish a Long Rest.

#### Perks

Group patrons offer characters perks ranging from a steady wage to access to libraries and magical resources to unexpected allies. Specific perks are presented in the description of each group patron.

### Option: Make Your Patron Mysterious

Concealing the true identity of your group patron can be a fun campaign linchpin, especially if your game involves magic-themed intrigue. Any of the magical group patrons described in this chapter might have reasons to stay anonymous. If you use a mysterious patron, create a strong reason the patron is hiding their identity.

#### High-Profile Patron

A group patron might remain anonymous because they're incredibly powerful, famous or infamous, or otherwise high profile, and their identity being known would undermine the characters' goals or ability to operate clandestinely. Or the patron might have reasons they wish to remain publicly unassociated with the characters. For example, perhaps the patron is a powerful political figure who can't publicly take sides in a civil dispute but wants the characters to side with freedom fighters over greedy merchants.

#### Ulterior Motives

![Powerful patrons are eager to sponsor young heroes, but their intent is sometimes unclear.](img/book/AU/035-03-008.powerful-patrons.webp)

On the other hand, a patron might hide their identity because their motives are malevolent. The patron might try to trick the characters into performing deeds that destabilize a fragile peace or empower an evil overlord. Or a patron might remain anonymous because the characters would never knowingly work for them, especially if the patron is nefarious and the heroes are upstanding.

>>*Kindness promotes peace and good, but the practicalities of the world require coin. A patron who supplies the latter is useful; a patron who also believes in you is invaluable.*— Alustriel

#### Concealing Their Identity

Mysterious magical patrons don't show their true faces to the characters, communicating via anonymous letters, prearranged signals, and networks of underlings similarly unaware of the patron's identity. A mysterious patron might refer to themself with a code name that fits the nature of the missions they ask the characters to undertake. In this case, the characters can't gain benefits associated with the patron's identity, such as the archmage's and esoteric institution's Documentation perks or the magical entity's Name Drop perk. Instead, the characters must find ways other than invoking their patron's name to achieve their goals.

***Using Magic.*** Since magical patrons are typically powerful mages, they use high-level spell effects to ensure the characters don't discover their identity. A patron might repeatedly use the *Programmed Illusion* spell to convey information to the characters via fictional, illusory agents. If a patron must interact with a character, the patron might use the *Seeming* spell to significantly alter their appearance. Similarly, a patron might use the *Mirage Arcane* spell to conceal their lair's true location behind an illusory waterfall or trees. As your characters gain power, ensure their patron uses magic that the characters are unlikely to dispel.

***Keeping the Characters on Board.*** If you use a mysterious group patron in your game, it's important for the characters to remain convinced that the patron's goals align with theirs. Attribute to the patron's code name deeds that are benevolent or helpful to the characters. Or ensure the patron compensates the characters well for their work, giving the characters a practical reason to continue working for them.

#### The Big Reveal

Eventually, the novelty of being sponsored by a mysterious group patron will wear thin. The characters might lose interest in working for someone unwilling to reveal their identity. They might begin to distrust or become frustrated with the patron.

When the time is right, you might reveal the patron's identity. You can do so suddenly, with the patron meeting the characters in person to thank them for their work, or more subtly.

***Dropping Hints.*** As the characters complete more of the patron's missions, the patron might begin to drop—intentionally or unintentionally—coded hints about their identity. Investigating these clues can become a fun side quest for the characters.

***Benevolent Patron.*** Once the characters have solved the clues, the patron might appear in person to congratulate them on discovering the secret. The patron might have intended to reveal themself once the characters proved themselves worthy through solving the clues, or the characters might have preempted the patron's plans. Either way, this gradual approach provides satisfaction to characters eager to learn their patron's identity.

***Malevolent Patron.*** If the patron's motives are malicious, the characters might react with horror to their patron's identity. In this case, providing a satisfying conclusion to your campaign involves the characters confirming their patron's identity and formulating a plan for dealing with them. That might involve leaving the patron's employ abruptly, turning the tables on the patron to discredit them, confronting the patron in combat, or another option.

Follow your players' lead in how to wrap up the campaign. You might decide that the patron anticipated the characters' realization, but keep in mind that defeating this duplicitous patron allows a satisfying conclusion.

### Archmage

As incredibly powerful spellcasters, archmages are capable of impressive deeds. Beyond slaying dragons or destroying undead, archmages have saved (and ruled!) entire cities, driven back armies, defeated the forces of the *Nine Hells* or the *Abyss*, and traveled in time. The mightiest archmages achieve magical mastery over the physical world. Some no longer age, spending centuries amassing unfathomable magical knowledge and prowess and creating spells they name after themselves.

With archmages' great learning and magical capabilities comes even greater willpower. Standing among the multiverse's strongest personalities, archmages personify stubbornness (even among the best) and self-centeredness (prominent among the worst). This unyielding nature translates into a voracious drive, whether it's to protect the weak, combat the wicked, amass the most knowledge, or take the biggest risks.

#### Archmage Motivations

**Archmages** might patronize a group for the following reasons:

- **Emissaries Are Needed.** **Archmages** amass allies and commune with powerful leaders during their travels throughout the multiverse. An archmage hires the characters to travel to these allies and request their aid—or aid those allies—in the archmage's pursuit of friendly relations or magical research.
- **Evil Is Rising.** Benevolent archmages recognize the sheer power of unchecked evil and are sensitive to such forces amassing strength or plotting terrible deeds. An archmage who discovers complex and wicked schemes hires the characters to complete tasks that undermine those schemes.
- **Lieutenants Are Required.** An archmage who identifies numerous threats plaguing a certain area hires the characters to face a host of lesser threats so the archmage can focus on powerful villainous leaders.

#### Archmage Patrons

The following characters from the D&D multiverse could serve as archmage group patrons:

![Lady Alustriel Silverhand is a powerful archmage who patronizes groups in the Forgotten Realms and beyond.](img/book/AU/036-03-009.alustriel-silverhand.webp)

- **Alustriel Silverhand.** One of the most powerful mages in the Forgotten Realms setting, Alustriel has been a proponent of good and enemy of evil for centuries. Alustriel is an immortal human daughter of Mystra, a god of magic, and ruled the city of Silverymoon for centuries. Alustriel maintains safe houses scattered across the planes for her allies as they promote compassion throughout the multiverse.
- **Vajra Safahr.** Chief mage of Waterdeep in the Forgotten Realms setting, the human Vajra is the youngest head in the history of Blackstaff Academy, a school for mages. While Vajra fends off threats to her city and her university, she also faces political conflicts from those who would unseat her from her place of prestige.
- **Yolande.** Known as the Faerie Queen, Yolande is the benevolent and beloved elf monarch of the nation of Celene in the world of Greyhawk. She is a mighty wizard who protects her nation against the incursion of Elemental Evil forces as well as threats against the Feywild and the Material Plane.

#### Creating Your Own Archmage

Instead of using one of the archmages described above, you can create an archmage to serve as a group patron for your characters. You might start by choosing one of the eight archmage stat blocks in chapter 6, each of which specializes in one school of magic, and add personality details as you see fit.

When creating an archmage, consider how close to the traditional archetype you want your archmage to be. A traditional archmage is incredibly powerful and eccentric. They're easily distracted because they're nurturing layers of complicated schemes. Your archmage could be very different: plain spoken, attentive to the characters, not as powerful as their reputation would suggest—or far more so.

When you create an archmage for your campaign, you can roll on or choose from the Archmage Type table to determine details of the archmage's persona.

##### Archmage Type
| 1d6 | Patron Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
|:---:|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Aspiring Tyrant. Secretly aspiring to immense political power, the archmage sends the characters on missions that covertly support the archmage's goal.                                                                                                                 |
|  2  | Disguised Sage. The archmage disguises themself as a humble apprentice of limited magical ability, while their loud and boastful student pretends to be the archmage. The two keep their true identities secret from the party.                                         |
|  3  | Distracted Genius. The archmage lives in a secluded cottage in the middle of the forest and spends most of their time talking to animals and plants. They employ the characters to handle problems so the mage can spend more time on their harmless personal pursuits. |
|  4  | Forlorn Lover. Having lost the love of their life through hubris to rejection or death, the archmage is focused on winning that individual back or resurrecting them. They need the characters to take care of other problems in the meanwhile.                         |
|  5  | Redeemed Demonologist. Once a devotee of a demon prince or archdevil, the archmage has renounced evil. They want the characters to solve the problems created when the archmage abandoned their former patron while the archmage focuses on larger challenges.          |
|  6  | Wizened Elf. The elf archmage is thousands of years old, their long life magically extended. The archmage remembers errors made over the centuries by well-meaning individuals and employs the characters to stop similar mistakes from happening again.                |

#### Archmage Patron Perks

Characters in a party whose patron is an archmage gain the following perks.

***Compensation.*** **Archmages** amass considerable wealth during their adventures and travels, and archmage patrons generously compensate their underlings. The nature of your work for an archmage influences how you're paid. On average, the archmage pays each member of your group 2 GP per day (enough to sustain a *Comfortable* lifestyle). Or you might be paid (at least 300 GP) each time you complete a task the archmage assigns.
