***Documentation.*** Each member of your group has identification noting their affiliation with the archmage, which carries clout in areas where the archmage is known. If the archmage is the ruler of a city or nation, the documentation grants the characters appropriate authority in that realm.

***Magical Resources.*** **Archmages** collect untold magic items and magical curiosities, though these collections are often scattered among far-flung sanctums. An archmage can give you any Common or Uncommon magic item in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*, as long as possession of the item would help your group's tasks. When your group reaches level 11, the archmage can retrieve one Rare and one Very Rare magic item in between the group's tasks and award it to you as additional compensation. When your group reaches level 17, the archmage can retrieve one Rare, Very Rare, or Legendary magic item in between tasks and award it to you as additional compensation.

***Spellcasting.*** **Archmages** can use their spellpower to help your missions. An archmage can cast any level 1 or lower spell for you at no cost, as long as the spell's effects would aid your work. The archmage can cast any level 2–8 spell for you at half the usual cost.

![Coins](img/book/AU/037-03-010.coins.webp)

#### Archmage Contacts

An archmage group patron might prioritize working with the characters, meeting with the party directly to deliver missions and collect reports. In this case, the archmage becomes a significant recurring character in your campaign, so give the archmage a manner of speaking or another feature that makes them distinctive. For example, the archmage might talk to their snake familiar, wear outrageously garish outfits, or hum odd tunes when distracted.

But archmages have a lot to do, and the patron might send a representative in their place. Roll on or choose from the Archmage Contact table to determine how the archmage interacts with the characters.

##### Archmage Contact
| 1d6 | Contract Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
|:---:|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Apprentice. A Humanoid apprentice relays messages to the characters. The apprentice might be harried, exceedingly proud, or exhausted and overworked.                                                                                                 |
|  2  | *Magic Mouth*. The archmage sends messages through the *Magic Mouth* spell, casting it on objects that vanish once the message is delivered.                                                                                                          |
|  3  | Magical Servant. The contact is a loyal servant creature. It might be a Beast or Plant awakened through the *Awaken* spell or a fantastical creature such as a **Blink Dog**, a **Couatl**, or a **Flumph**.                                          |
|  4  | Projected Illusion. The archmage communicates with the characters through the *Project Image* spell. The characters might never see the archmage in person.                                                                                           |
|  5  | Sentient Magic Item. A sentient weapon, helmet, or other magic item communicates with the characters on the archmage's behalf. The item is hundreds of years old and exasperated with the characters' mistakes, though it loves its archmage creator. |
|  6  | *Simulacrum*. The archmage uses the *Simulacrum* spell to create simulacra that issue instructions on the archmage's behalf.                                                                                                                          |

#### An Archmage Campaign

A campaign driven by an archmage patron might unfold according to this outline:

- **Levels 1–4.** As the characters begin their adventuring career, the archmage watches them from afar, monitoring their activities and subtly directing their attention to the groundlings (see chapter 6) that a wicked rival archmage commands.
- **Levels 5–10.** The archmage reveals themself to the characters and hires the characters to investigate the rival archmage, whose schemes threaten the city or surrounding realm. You can use the stat block for one of the archmages in chapter 6 for this rival, whom the party confronts in a climactic battle.
- **Levels 11–16.** The archmage patron sends the characters all over the world to gather magical secrets from creatures that hoard knowledge; adult dragons, devils, and a **Demilich** make suitable antagonists.
- **Levels 17–20.** The archmage patron retires. The characters are now obliged to deal with the archmage's enemies and rivals while exploring the archmage's extraplanar lair.

### Esoteric Institution

An esoteric institution might be a magical academy, an order of monks researching lost magical techniques, or any other organization focused on the study, preservation, and use of magical lore. Institutions usually maintain a base of operations such as a campus, monastery, or remote fortress. The institution might have a large staff including administrators, teachers, groundskeepers, and guards. In addition to an excellent library, it probably has a secure vault for storing dangerous magic items, and more than one Artifact might be housed there.

Esoteric institutions have a long history that stretches back generations. In that time, an institution has made enemies and collected magic items that have been stored away and forgotten. The institution's headquarters might have secret chambers protected by puzzles and traps. Despite its reclusive or insular nature, the institution monitors the world beyond its walls. When it finds magic being used for evil or learns of long-forgotten lore to be recovered, it sends adventurers on missions.

![The Tower of High Sorcery in the world of Dragonlance charges heroes with protecting magical secrets from a destructive draconic army.](img/book/AU/038-03-011.tower-of-high-sorcery.webp)

#### Esoteric Institution Motivations

An esoteric institution might patronize a group for the following reasons:

- **The Characters Are Students.** The characters are students at an academy such as the Tower of High Sorcery in the Dragonlance setting or Strixhaven University and are sent on missions as part of their studies. The institution doesn't intentionally send the characters on dangerous missions, and it sends rescue teams if the characters get into trouble while pursuing goals tied to the academy.
- **The Institution Needs Field Agents.** An esoteric institution includes sages, scholars, and researchers, but few warriors and explorers. The institution wants to hire the characters for missions deemed too dangerous for its own staff, such as rescuing students in peril.
- **The Characters Are Specialists.** An esoteric institution seeks out the characters because the characters are experts in a particular field relevant to a current problem or interest. For example, if the characters recently defeated a powerful vampire, the institution hires them to recover that vampire's hidden library of magical tomes.

#### Esoteric Institution Patrons

The following organizations from the D&D multiverse could serve as esoteric institution group patrons:

- **Order of the Guardians.** The Order of the Guardians is a group of ascetics who gather, sequester, and protect dangerous magic in the Ravenloft setting. Its members maintain several hidden and well-protected monasteries, but the largest is Watchers' Stronghold in the Mistlands, a piece of the disintegrating domain of Darkon. The order is stretched thin, outnumbered and outmatched by the horrors found in the *Domains of Dread*, and it's quick to hire adventurers willing to venture into dangerous sites to recover powerful items or spells so they can't be used for evil. More information on the Order of the Guardians and Darkon can be found in *Ravenloft: The Horrors Within*.
- **Thebestys.** In the Outlands of the Planescape setting, the walled city of Thebestys is home to an ibis-headed, many-named god of learning. This god maintains a great library at the city's center. The library enjoys a reputation as a matchless repository of knowledge. The ibis-headed god employs the characters as agents who travel through the city of *Sigil* and the planes to find books and bring them back to the library for copying and collection. Thebestys and the Outlands are detailed in *Planescape: Adventures in the Multiverse*.
- **Tower of High Sorcery.** In the Dragonlance setting, there were once five Towers of High Sorcery, where mages were trained. Due to disaster and tragedy, only one remains. There, spellcasters study magic in traditions tied to the world of Krynn's three moons. Characters in this setting serve the mages of the tower as teachers, students, or defenders, and they are dispatched to locations across the world to preserve magical secrets from the destructive draconian army. You can find more information on the Tower of High Sorcery in *Dragonlance: Shadow of the Dragon Queen*.

#### Creating Your Own Esoteric Institution

Instead of using one of the esoteric institutions described above, you can create an institution to serve as a group patron for your characters. This gives you an opportunity to customize the institution to appeal to your players and to reflect the characters they've created. For example, if your players have made wilderness-oriented characters such as Barbarians, Druids, and Rangers, the institution you create is likely focused on preserving nature. If you want tension between the characters and the institution, the leaders of the institution might be of different alignments than the characters.

When you create an esoteric institution for your campaign, roll on or choose from the Esoteric Institution Type table to inspire the institution's details.

##### Esoteric Institution Type
| 1d6 | Patron Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
|:---:|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Apocalyptic Cult. This cult collects powerful magic lore and items to prepare for a world-ending battle the institution's leaders know is coming. These leaders don't tell the characters the full context of their plans, but they need the characters' help in preparing for the fight.                                                              |
|  2  | Magical Warehouse. This institution is dedicated to finding and hiding dangerous magic. It maintains a warehouse filled with anonymous crates, each containing a powerful magic item or tome of magical lore. The institution needs the characters to track down and recover dangerous items to store in the warehouse, which itself might be magical. |
|  3  | Military School. At this institution, mages train to serve in the realm's armed forces as officers and mobile artillery units. The institution emphasizes obedience, duty, and patriotism, and it needs disciplined characters to perform missions to ensure its longevity.                                                                            |
|  4  | Religious Order. A religious order devoted to one or more gods of magic teaches spellcasting, performs religious rituals for the community, and pursues the gods' goals. The institution needs the characters to further these goals.                                                                                                                  |
|  5  | Underground Society. This informal network of aristocrats, mages, and scholars meets in inns, taverns, and other social venues to pursue clandestine schemes that might include sabotage, thievery, and mayhem. The institution needs discreet characters to perform delicate tasks.                                                                   |
|  6  | University. Students from all over the world come to this famous university to study magic. The institution needs the characters to perform missions to preserve and further its reputation.                                                                                                                                                           |

#### Esoteric Institution Patron Perks

Characters in a party whose patron is an esoteric institution gain the following perks.

***Compensation.*** The institution pays for the work you do on its behalf. The nature of your employment influences your compensation. On average, the institution pays each member of your group 1 GP per day (or enough to sustain a *Modest* lifestyle). Or you might be paid a bounty (at least 250 GP) for each magic item or piece of lore you bring back from your adventures and give to the institution.

***Documentation.*** Each member of your group has identification noting their affiliation with the institution, which carries clout in areas where the institution is known. For magical academies such as the Tower of High Sorcery or Strixhaven, this is a letter on official stationery marked with the academy's seal, but for secretive organizations like the Order of the Guardians, the documentation takes the form of a signet ring or brooch marked with a discreet symbol.

***Esoteric Research.*** Esoteric institutions include expansive libraries specializing in magical knowledge. When you take a *Bastion* turn, you can (as a group) issue the Research order to the institution as if it were a Library facility in your Bastion. The topic chosen must be related to magic in some way; at the DM's discretion, the institution can research topics related to the planes as well. Once you reach level 13, you instead can issue the Research order to the institution as if it were an Archive.

***Scroll and Spellbook Access.*** You have access to the institution's library of *Spell Scrolls* and spellbooks, as well as supplies for copying spells. You can buy any *Spell Scroll* from the institution for half its usual value, as indicated in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*. You can find any spell from the Player's Handbook in the library, and the institution pays half the GP cost of copying the spell.

***Training.*** You have access to continuing education at no cost. You can spend 30 days in classes to gain one of the following benefits of your choice:

- **Skill.** You gain proficiency in one of these skills of your choice: Arcana, History, Nature, or Religion. You can gain proficiency in only one skill in this way.
- **Tool.** You gain proficiency with a tool of your choice.
- **Language.** You learn a language of your choice.

#### Esoteric Institution Contacts

An esoteric institution is typically large enough that the characters don't know everyone in it, and its leaders are too busy to interact with the characters directly. Usually, one individual is the party's contact, passing on instructions from the institution.

Think carefully about the contact's personality, because your players will interact with them often. The contact doesn't have to stay the same throughout the campaign; the institution might assign a new contact every mission or only in extreme circumstances.

Roll on or choose from the Esoteric Institution Contact table to determine who manages the relationship between the characters and the institution.

##### Esoteric Institution Contact
| 1d6 | Contact                                                                                                                                                                               |
|:---:|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Administrative Assistant. This polished professional has served the institution for decades, seems to know everything, and brooks no arguments.                                       |
|  2  | Anonymous Mail. The characters get their missions via couriers sent by the institution. The group patron might be a single individual pretending to speak for the entire institution. |
|  3  | Mascot. A highly intelligent creature beloved by the staff bonds with the party and serves as an adviser and guide.                                                                   |
|  4  | New Hire. Newly hired, the contact is inexperienced and can't answer the party's questions without consulting their superiors, but they're well meaning and sincere.                  |
|  5  | Overeager Graduate Student. This idealistic, advanced student idolizes the party and supports their every action.                                                                     |
|  6  | Senior Faculty Member. The most famous member of the institution personally delivers all missions and takes great interest in the party's activities.                                 |

#### An Esoteric Institution Campaign

A campaign driven by an esoteric institution patron might unfold according to this outline:

- **Levels 1–4.** The characters begin as students at an esoteric institution where they study magic. Their early adventures involve exploring the institution (overcoming animated objects and other guardians) and the surrounding region (perhaps populated by modrons, myconids, or undead). As part of their graduation exam, the characters are tasked to defeat a **Bulette** that threatens a nearby village.
- **Levels 5–10.** Now graduates of the institution, the characters are sent on missions to dangerous, far-off places where magical lore is hidden or being used for evil. They battle cultists of an evil god of magic and infiltrate the lair of a young dragon or a nest of **mind flayers**.
- **Levels 11–16.** Eventually the characters are offered senior positions at the institution, where they become teachers of a new generation of pupils. They must defend the institution against enemies such as **Arcanaloths**, **Rakshasas**, and a **Vampire Umbral Lord**.
- **Levels 17–20.** At the climax of the campaign, a traitorous member of the esoteric institution steals an item the party recovered earlier and tries to use it to attain vast power or simple vengeance. The traitor is being manipulated by a **Pit Fiend**, and only the characters can stop them.

### Magical Entity

A magical entity is a powerful creature of mysterious or otherworldly origin. The entity might openly rule the realm in which they live, or they might be a mysterious figure that manipulates events from behind the scenes. Regardless, the magical entity is old, extremely knowledgeable, and frightening.

The magical entity is surrounded by legends and superstition. Villagers and townsfolk in the region know the entity by name. Most magical entities are feared as monsters, even if they're benevolent, though some are revered as honorable creatures that protect the realm.

#### Magical Entity Motivations

Magical entities might patronize a group for the following reasons:

- **Bound to a Place.** The magical entity is bound to a particular location and can't leave. While supremely powerful and all but omniscient in their own realm, the being needs the characters to accomplish objectives beyond the entity's reach.
- **Like Recognizes Like.** The magical entity sees something in the characters that reminds the entity of themself. A good patron likely admires heroes who are kind and just, while an evil patron might be impressed by characters they perceive as proud and cruel.
- **Too Famous to Travel.** The magical entity is so well known—and feared—that they can't travel without attracting unwanted attention and alerting their enemies. The entity needs agents who can avoid notice.

#### Magical Entity Patrons

![Bahamut, a patron of good dragons, enlists heroes to fight against terrible evils.](img/book/AU/039-03-012.bahamut.webp)

The following beings from the D&D multiverse could serve as magical entity patrons:

- **Baba Yaga.** An arch-hag known as the Mother of All Witches, Baba Yaga travels the multiverse in her chicken-legged hut, pursuing inscrutable goals. She's also the adoptive mother of the famous witch *Tasha*. Baba Yaga might hire characters of all alignments to seek esoteric magical knowledge or to act as her emissaries in formal political environments. Her goals and motivations are mysterious, but she offers great power to those she favors.
- **Bahamut.** *Bahamut* is a god of good in numerous settings and is like a king to metallic dragons. He appears to mortals in various guises, often accompanied by seven yellow canaries that are gold dragons in disguise. In the world of Dragonlance, he has appeared as Fizban the Fabulous, a bumbling mage whose dramatic "accidents" often have hidden purposes. Bahamut is an excellent patron for good-aligned groups; he's unlikely to work with evil characters unless he thinks they can be convinced to change their ways. He's likely to conceal his true nature from the characters.
- **Strahd von Zarovich.** Trapped in the domain of Barovia, the vampire *Strahd* is described in *Ravenloft: The Horrors Within* and *Curse of Strahd*. Strahd wields supreme power in his homeland, but he can't leave it. He needs the characters to be his eyes and ears outside Barovia. Supremely egotistical, Strahd finds the idea of concealing his true identity entertaining for a while, but once the party has done a task or two for him, the mask comes off and the vampire reveals himself. Knowingly working with Strahd for an extended time likely won't appeal to most heroic parties, though Strahd could make a great ongoing patron for amoral adventurers.

#### Creating Your Own Magical Entity

Instead of using one of the magical entities described above, you can create an entity to serve as a group patron for your characters. You might start by skimming through the *Monster Manual* looking for intelligent monsters with a CR of 13 or higher.

Even if your magical entity is frightening, strange, or otherworldly, that doesn't mean the entity must be evil. Creatures commonly associated with evil deeds—such as chromatic dragons or undead—might have a change of heart and shift to a neutral or good alignment. But even a good magical entity can come across as weird, remote, or unsettling. Ordinary folk flee from the entity, but the characters are brave enough to interact with them.

When you create a magical entity for your campaign, you can roll on or choose from the Magical Entity Type table to determine the entity's nature.

##### Magical Entity Type
| 1d6 | Patron Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
|:---:|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Angel. This powerful Celestial from the Upper Planes disguises itself to mingle among ordinary people and seeks virtuous characters to join its fight against evil.                                                                  |
|  2  | Beast Lord. An immortal, legendary spirit in animal form, such as an **Animal Lord**, protects the environment and its inhabitants. The spirit needs emissaries as well as strike teams to combat threats to the land.               |
|  3  | Ancient Dragon. A chromatic dragon who has turned away from evil behavior or a metallic dragon who champions good recruits the characters for their valiant heroics.                                                                 |
|  4  | Giant. A **Storm Giant** leader who lives in a flying castle high above the realm, hidden by clouds or a perpetual storm, has a vested interest in matters on the ground and sponsors the characters to act as the giant's emissary. |
|  5  | Reformed Rakshasa. A Lawful Good **Rakshasa** works layered schemes against other Fiends and recruits the characters to further its mission.                                                                                         |
|  6  | Undying Steward. A powerful mage helped found the realm and is thought long dead, but they have survived as a **Baelnorn** or a **Taisus** (see chapter 6 for both).                                                                 |

#### Magical Entity Patron Perks

Characters in a party whose patron is a magical entity gain the following perks.

***Compensation.*** The magical entity pays for the work you do on their behalf. For every mission you successfully perform for the entity, you are rewarded with treasure worth 250 GP (or more, at the DM's discretion). Your patron might reward you with Common or Uncommon magic items, especially consumables such as potions and *Spell Scrolls*.

***Friends in Strange Places.*** Monsters of the same creature type as the magical entity are Friendly to you by default.

***Name Drop.*** You can reveal the name of your group patron to your enemies, filling them with trepidation. When you make a Charisma (Intimidation) check to influence a creature, you can name your group patron, granting you Advantage on the check. If that check succeeds, you then gain a bonus to all checks made to influence that creature for 1 hour; the bonus is equal to half your level. Once you use this benefit, you can't do so again until 7 days have passed.

#### Magical Entity Contacts

A magical entity is typically reclusive. Their unusual appearance and nature mean that they can't simply walk down the streets of a settlement or meet the characters in a tavern—at least, not without an excellent disguise. For this reason, a magical entity is likely to communicate with the party through an intermediary or magical effect. But these contacts and effects, like the entity, should be unsettling or even scary.

Roll on or choose from the Magical Entity Contact table to determine how the magical entity interacts with the characters.

##### Magical Entity Contact
| 1d6 | Contact                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
|:---:|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Awakened Beasts. One or more awakened Beasts deliver the details of the characters' missions. The characters might consistently meet with an awakened Beast quest giver, or they might receive each quest from a different creature.                        |
|  2  | Enigmatic Dreams. The entity communicates with the characters through the *Dream* spell, sending them strange dreams suggesting specific courses of action.                                                                                                 |
|  3  | Lackey. A **Commoner** servant attends the entity and delivers messages to the characters along with dire warnings of the consequences of failure.                                                                                                          |
|  4  | Mirror Reflections. The entity communicates to the characters via mirrors using illusions. The entity appears as the reflection of one of the characters, or the entity's image replaces that of a character.                                               |
|  5  | Prophecy. The entity communicates indirectly by spreading prophecies to those nearby. The characters are expected to undertake missions that would fulfill the prophecies.                                                                                  |
|  6  | Shape-Shifter. The entity never openly appears to the characters, but creatures that claim to be the entity's servants deliver the characters' assignments. These creatures are the entity in disguise, using the *Shapechange* spell or a similar ability. |

#### A Magical Entity Campaign

A campaign driven by a magical entity patron might unfold according to this outline:

- **Levels 1–4.** The campaign begins as the characters stumble into the entity's domain and prove themselves against creatures the entity considers enemies. Aberrations such as **Grells**, **Gricks**, and **Grimlocks** are all thematically appropriate foes.
- **Levels 5–10.** The magical entity approaches the characters or invites them to the entity's lair and offers to become their group patron, sending them on missions against monstrous foes. Githyanki and slaadi make good antagonists for this tier of play.
- **Levels 11–16.** The mysterious mastermind controlling these Aberrations—whom the characters haven't yet identified—captures or imprisons the magical entity, perhaps through the use of an Artifact, and the characters must rescue them. The characters battle **Aboleths**, **Beholders**, **Death Tyrants**, and **Mind Flayer Arcanists**.
- **Levels 17–20.** The mysterious mastermind is revealed as a creature similar to the group patron, but different in nature. For example, Bahamut is captured by a group of **Ancient Red Dragons**. Or Strahd is at the mercy of a powerful **Lich**, such as Acererak or Vecna. These two liches are further described in *Vecna: Eve of Ruin*, including game statistics for Vecna.

### Political Faction

A political faction group patron is tied to the governing structure of a magical realm. A political faction usually has great wealth and access to the realm's resources, but the faction leaders are politicians and bureaucrats, not adventurers. Even if the leaders are powerful mages, they have no interest in risking their own lives. Instead, they hire the characters as spies or agents.

Campaigns with a political faction group patron combine high magic with political intrigue. The characters might be kept in the dark by their patron faction, which gives them only the information they absolutely need (and sometimes not even that). The faction sends the party into dangerous situations that force them to improvise.

#### Political Faction Motivations

A political faction might patronize a group for the following reasons:

- **The Characters Are Residents.** If the characters live in the realm where the faction governs, the faction believes the characters are easier to recruit and more likely to be loyal. The faction might exert legal pressure on the characters, forcing them to do as the faction requires.
- **The Characters Are Foreign.** The faction prefers to hire outsiders who can operate in neighboring realms without attracting attention, and who know the languages and customs of the faction's rivals.
- **The Characters Are Deniable Assets.** The political faction hires the adventures without official sanction. The party is sent on dangerous missions in far-off lands, and if they're captured, the political faction denies any connection or responsibility for the party's actions.

#### Political Faction Patrons

![The Red Wizards of Thay are a dangerous and powerful group patron.](img/book/AU/040-03-013.red-wizards-of-thay.webp)

The following organizations, each detailed in *Forgotten Realms: Heroes of Faerûn*, could serve as political faction group patrons:

- **Red Wizards.** The Red Wizards rule Thay, a land on the eastern edge of Faerûn in the Forgotten Realms. Thay is expansionist, and its rulers are mostly cruel tyrants. Thay and the Red Wizards are expanded on in *Arcana Unleashed: Deadfall*, which includes an adventure that embroils characters in a mission to kill *Szass Tam*, a Red Wizard lich who plots to once again control the nation.
- **Simbarch Council.** Thay's neighbor, the land of Aglarond, is ruled by a group of sorcerers called the Simbarch Council. In addition to governing the realm, the council trains sorcerers who come to Aglarond from across Faerûn. Aglarond has a long history of military conflict with Thay.
- **Witches of Rashemen.** Rashemen is a land on Thay's northern border. Rashemen is ruled by masked warlocks known as Wychlaran, who make pacts with ancient spirits that dwell in Rashemen's dense and magical forests. Like Aglarond, Rashemen has been invaded repeatedly by Thay, and some of the witches occasionally wish to strike at their longtime enemies.

#### Creating Your Own Political Faction

Instead of using one of the political factions described above, you can create a political faction to serve as a group patron for your characters.

Political factions have a long history in the realms where they appear, and they're distinguished by symbols, mottoes, and pass phrases. They require an oath, though some members of the faction are more loyal to that oath than others. Give some thought to the faction's history and how its fortunes have risen or fallen over time. Give your faction at least one rival group in the same realm or a neighboring one; many adventures the characters undertake should be missions against a rival.

When you create a political faction for your campaign, you can roll on or choose from the Political Faction Type table to determine the faction's nature.

##### Political Faction Type
| 1d6 | Patron Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
|:---:|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Environmentalists. The faction seeks to magically preserve the realm's natural spaces and wildlife. It doesn't receive funding and resources from the government, so it recruits the characters to act as its agents.                                                 |
|  2  | Magical Intelligence Agency. This government agency, which protects the realm from magical threats, needs the characters to investigate cases and neutralize dangers.                                                                                                 |
|  3  | Magical Military. The characters are soldiers in the realm's army. They're given orders by individuals of higher rank who expect to be obeyed.                                                                                                                        |
|  4  | Rebel Faction. The faction opposes the current rulers of the realm and operates from a secret base. The characters are tasked with gathering resources and allies for a coming revolt, though if the characters are caught, the rebels disavow all knowledge of them. |
|  5  | Ruling Party. The faction is in power due to its members' magical prowess. The faction needs the characters to sniff out threats to ensure the nation's safety—and the faction's political supremacy.                                                                 |
|  6  | Shadow Cabinet. The shadow cabinet operates clandestinely. The characters know the truth and must keep their missions secret lest the rulers discover and disband the faction.                                                                                        |

#### Political Faction Patron Perks

Characters in a party whose patron is a political faction gain the following perks.

***Expenses and Salary.*** Your patron reimburses you for expenses incurred as part of your work. You are required to account for your expenses and must explain any extraordinary expenditures, but routine travel, ordinary equipment, and basic services don't draw a second glance.

Additionally, your employment brings an income of 1 GP per day (enough to sustain a *Modest* lifestyle). At the DM's discretion, your salary might increase or decrease depending on the faction's nature, the type of work, and the length of your employment.

***Immunity.*** As long as you remain in the political faction's good graces, you can avoid prosecution for minor offenses under the laws of the realm where the faction is based. However, committing serious crimes, especially if they're unrelated to assigned work, is a sure way to fall out of your patron's good graces.

***Powerful Allies.*** The political faction has secured alliances with powerful businesspeople, spellcasters, and tradespeople. The faction can call upon these allies to secure transportation for you by a means of your choosing (via coach, ship, or teleportation) at half the usual cost. When you take a Bastion turn, if you are level 9+, you can (as a group) issue the Recruit order to the political faction as if the faction was a Teleportation Circle facility in your Bastion. If you are level 17+, you can instead issue the Recruit order to the political faction as if it were a Guildhall facility within your Bastion.

#### Political Faction Contacts

A political faction has well-established procedures establishing how the characters communicate with their superiors, get to adventure sites, and return home when the mission is over. Procedures include rules for disguising communications, such as leaving messages in dead drops or protecting reports with the *Illusory Script* spell.

A political faction is large enough to include a cast of recurring characters, and the party might have a different contact for every mission. Alternatively, the party's contact might change when a shift in government or evolving political philosophy forces the faction to adapt.

Roll on or choose from the Political Faction Contact table to determine who manages the relationship between the characters and the political faction.

##### Political Faction Contact
| 1d6 | Contact Type                                                                                                                                                                                    |
|:---:|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Excitable Artificer. The contact is eager for the characters to test their latest inventions—which are often unsafe.                                                                            |
|  2  | Harried Bureaucrat. This administrator has a busy schedule and lacks the time or resources to supervise every task. They assign missions in a rush and leave the party to sort out the details. |
|  3  | Naive Intellectual. This reclusive intellectual with encyclopedic knowledge but no field expertise suggests impractical or unrealistic courses of action.                                       |
|  4  | Political Officer. Responsible for prioritizing the faction's political agenda, the officer frequently questions the characters' loyalty.                                                       |
|  5  | Public Relations Expert. The contact magically manipulates public perception and strives to make the characters popular.                                                                        |
|  6  | Well-Mannered Aristocrat. This genteel individual personally meets with the characters to give them their missions, usually over tea in their magic-filled abode.                               |

#### A Political Faction Campaign

A campaign driven by a political faction patron might unfold according to this outline:

- **Levels 1–4.** The characters are recruited by intermediaries and are unaware they're serving the political faction. They receive a mission to infiltrate a rival faction and steal the spellbook of a well-protected **Mage**.
- **Levels 5–10.** Having proven themselves, the characters are brought further into the faction and learn their employer's identity. New missions send them to rival nations, where they sabotage rituals, steal magic items, plant incriminating evidence, and thwart plots to assassinate members of their patron faction.
- **Levels 11–16.** The characters learn about a plot by a rival faction to assemble or retrieve an Artifact capable of great destruction in the realm where the patron faction is based. Retrieving and destroying the Artifact leads the characters on a grand adventure that visits multiple planes of existence.
- **Levels 17–20.** A sudden and overwhelming strike by a rival faction eliminates most of the highest-ranking members of the patron faction, and the characters ascend into those positions. Now they must decide how to respond to the attack and settle the rivalry once and for all.

### Trickster

Tricksters are powerful beings—often archfey—who are known for their whimsical nature. A trickster can take many forms and might appear pleasant, angry, or simply otherworldly. But at their core, the trickster is unpredictable, their priorities and goals changing with the seasons or even from day to day.

Tricksters make good patrons for groups that prefer social interaction, exploration, and nonviolent conflict resolution over combat. Many tricksters prefer humiliating and pranking their enemies to killing them. Groups with a trickster patron are likely to spend time in the *Feywild*, visiting Domains of Delight and learning the distinctive rules of each.

#### Trickster Motivations

A trickster might patronize a group for the following reasons:

- **The Characters Are Interesting.** Tricksters are like magpies, driven to collect shiny baubles. Adventurers are colorful, larger-than-life personalities who are likely to attract a trickster's attention.
- **The Trickster Needs Plausible Deniability.** A trickster makes enemies, and those enemies often instantly recognize the trickster and are alert to disguises. The trickster needs agents who aren't known to be associated with the trickster.
- **The Characters Are Expendable Assets.** The trickster needs dangerous tasks accomplished and is unwilling to send their more experienced agents out of concern those agents might not return. The characters' deaths won't inconvenience the trickster.

![Tasha is a trickster patron whose myriad alliances shroud her motivations in mystery.](img/book/AU/041-03-014.tasha.webp)

#### Trickster Patrons

The following characters from the D&D multiverse, all briefly described in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*, could serve as trickster patrons:

- **Queen of Air and Darkness.** The Queen of Air and Darkness is a foreboding and mysterious archfey who rules the Gloaming Court, a Domain of Delight in the *Feywild*. She favors followers who are willful and follow their instincts, obeying no laws. She's likely to give the characters total freedom to choose how to accomplish their missions.
- **Summer Queen.** Titania, the Summer Queen, is a benevolent figure, but she nonetheless displays all the whimsical arbitrariness of other archfey. The Summer Court is infamous for its elaborate rules of behavior; characters working for Titania inevitably break unstated rules, prompting Titania to assign them punishments that lead to further adventures.
- **Tasha.** *Tasha*, the adopted daughter of Baba Yaga, is a mighty spellcaster who authored the *Demonomicon* and invented several famous spells. Tasha has a unique relationship with fate, having been a tyrant known as Iggwilv, the Witch Queen of Perrenland; a planes-walking archmage; and Zybilna, archfey of the domain of Prismeer. You can learn more about Tasha in *The Wild Beyond the Witchlight*. Her mysterious goals could lead the characters anywhere in the multiverse.

#### Creating Your Own Trickster

Instead of using one of the tricksters described above, you can create a trickster to serve as a group patron for your characters.

A trickster group patron is whimsical, entertaining, and funny. However, a little whimsy goes a long way. Your trickster should focus their practical jokes and pranks on rivals and enemies, not the characters, or the party will soon find the trickster tiresome rather than amusing. Ensure that the trickster (mostly) tells the truth to the characters while maintaining a sense of humor and indulging the characters' pranks and jests.

When you create a trickster for your campaign, you can roll on or choose from the Trickster Type table to determine their nature.

##### Trickster Type
| 1d6 | Patron Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
|:---:|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Bored Immortal. This being has lived for centuries or perhaps millennia and has seen it all. They desperately crave entertainment and generously pay anyone who provides spectacle—the more dangerous, the better.                                                                                                    |
|  2  | Child Prodigy. A young child of incredibly powerful magical beings, the prodigy has inherited great power but doesn't yet have the maturity to use it wisely. The prodigy uses their force of will and considerable monetary resources to convince the characters to undertake their capricious missions.             |
|  3  | God of Mischief. A god devoted to mischief and deception conceals their identity from the party while sending the characters on missions to humiliate the god's rivals.                                                                                                                                               |
|  4  | Long-Forgotten **Archmage**. This ancient archmage, long thought dead, possesses great power but is increasingly eccentric and bitter that no one remembers their most glorious past deeds. The archmage hires the characters to fool individuals from the archmage's past into making amends for perceived misdeeds. |
|  5  | Puckish Fey. This powerful fey serves the archfey ruler of a Domain of Delight as a court jester or is themself an archfey. The fey delights in sending the characters on well-paid missions that further the trickster's schemes or simply amuse them.                                                               |
|  6  | Willful Hag. A hag coven member doesn't approve of deeds performed by the other hags in the coven and assigns the characters missions to secretly undermine the rival hags.                                                                                                                                           |

#### Trickster Patron Perks

Characters in a party whose patron is a trickster gain the following perks.

***Compensation.*** A trickster group patron pays you as a way of binding you to them and ensuring your continued loyalty. On average, the trickster pays each member of your group 4 GP per day (enough to sustain a *Wealthy* lifestyle). They might bestow additional gifts such as gemstones, art objects, or Common or Uncommon magic items on you on completion of a successful mission. However, the trickster might withhold this money and reclaim gifts if you displease them or refuse an onerous mission.

***Fickle Friends.*** The trickster has formed many surprising relationships in their travels across the multiverse, and they can call upon unexpected allies to aid your missions. These allies are often otherworldly—including devils, demons, fey, and stranger creatures—but they are always Friendly while aiding you (though not necessarily afterward). The trickster can call on these friends to secure transportation for you by a means of your choosing (via coach, ship, or teleportation) at half the usual cost. When you take a Bastion turn, if you are level 9+, you can (as a group) issue the Recruit order to the trickster as if they were a Teleportation Circle facility in your Bastion.

***Resources.*** The trickster has a vast collection of dangerous relics, magic items, spellbooks, and other resources collected over a long lifetime. You can call in a favor to gain access to these resources. Additionally, you can consult with the trickster or their more powerful associates, if you're willing to risk their attention.

***Strange Gifts.*** The trickster grants your group a small measure of esoteric power. At level 5, and again at level 13, you each gain one supernatural gift as described in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*. The DM determines which supernatural gifts are available.

#### Trickster Contacts

Deception is key to the trickster group patron, and a trickster is likely to meet with the characters personally while concealing their identity. But not all tricksters can disguise themselves, and the disguise falls away once the trickster finds the joke has worn stale. Tricksters instead might communicate with the characters through various other means, including agents, spells, or magic items.

If the trickster uses an agent, roll on or choose from the Trickster Contact table to determine who manages the relationship between the characters and the trickster.

##### Trickster Contact
| 1d6 | Contact Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
|:---:|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Bound by Debt. A creature the trickster saved from a terrible fate brings the characters messages to work off the debt incurred by this rescue.                                                                                              |
|  2  | Bound Demon. A demon the trickster bound long ago is magically prevented from doing evil and satisfies itself with pranking the party and encouraging them to solve problems with violence.                                                  |
|  3  | Faerie Dragon. The trickster's **Faerie Dragon Youth** attendant occasionally slips away to give advice and gifts to the characters. The trickster might be unaware of the faerie dragon's mercurial actions.                                |
|  4  | Minuscule Fey. The contact, a **Pixie** or **Sprite**, is desperately in love with the trickster and considers the party to be the means by which the contact can earn the trickster's affection.                                            |
|  5  | Transformed Enemy. The trickster overcame a hated rival long ago and transformed the rival into a sentient magic item, a talking animal, or another form. Now the rival delivers messages to the party and warns them against the trickster. |
|  6  | Wise Centaur. This wise, patient, and elderly **Centaur Warden** has trained generations of adventurers. The centaur doesn't always agree with the trickster but respects their power.                                                       |

#### A Trickster Campaign

A campaign driven by a trickster patron might unfold according to this outline:

- **Levels 1–4.** The trickster recruits the characters while concealing their own identity and tests the characters by pitting them against lesser Fey such as bullywugs, goblins, and **worgs** in service to a coven of **Green Hags**.
- **Levels 5–10.** The characters have earned the grudging respect of the trickster, who sends them into the Feywild on missions that emphasize exploration and interaction. The party is tasked with stealing a bauble the trickster favors or persuading two of the trickster's enemies to fight each other.
- **Levels 11–16.** The trickster's enemies catch up with them, and the characters must protect their patron from demons, adult dragons, and mortal archmages, all of whom have longstanding grudges against the trickster.
- **Levels 17–20.** The characters discover the enemies fighting the trickster are being subtly manipulated by a villainous **Arch-hag** that seeks to supplant the trickster and seize their role. To end the threat, the characters must corner the hag in its lair.

### Wizards' Circle

Powerful spellcasters occasionally form an alliance known as a wizards' circle to pursue goals beyond the reach of any individual. Wizards' circles are often temporary, formed to resolve a specific problem that threatens the world or the multiverse. But even archmages need friends or allies, and the members of a wizards' circle might make their alliance permanent.

In many ways, a wizards' circle is like an archmage group patron, with the added complication that the circle consists of several strong-willed individuals who don't necessarily like each other. The characters get caught in the middle of long debates and old rivalries between members of the circle, and it can be hard to satisfy one member of the circle without alienating another.

#### Wizards' Circle Motivations

A wizards' circle might patronize a group for the following reasons:

- **The Characters Balance Each Other.** Each member of the wizards' circle has their own preferences when selecting agents and operatives. The party represents a collection of these preferences, with each member of the circle backing one member of the party. Although each character is backed by a single member, the circle as a whole serves as a group patron for the characters.
- **The Characters Have Special Skills.** A wizards' circle lacks expertise in fields outside members' specialties, including types of magic outside their bailiwick, martial prowess, or exploration skills. The characters provide useful abilities that the circle members lack.
- **Pawns on the Chessboard.** The wizards' circle supports the group because the characters feature in the circle's long-term plans. The circle members might know one of the characters is unknowingly a noble and thus a potential candidate for the throne, or they might have identified one of the characters as the only person who can safely wield a dangerous Artifact. These facts might not be known to the characters.

![Notoriously argumentative mages Jallarzi Sallavarian, Bigby, and Mordenkainen unite when a dire threat to their world arises.](img/book/AU/042-03-015.jallarzi-bigby-and-mordenkainen.webp)

#### Wizards' Circle Patrons

The following organizations could serve as wizards' circle group patrons:

- **Circle of Eight.** The Circle of Eight is an alliance of powerful spellcasters that includes the gnome *Bigby*; the humans *Jallarzi Sallavarian*, *Mordenkainen*, and *Otiluke*; and the dwarf *Otto*, all briefly described in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*. The Circle is based in the world of Greyhawk and concerns itself with maintaining the power balance between Oerth's realms and factions. The Circle of Eight favors neutral-aligned agents and avoids attention, preferring to work behind the scenes.
- **Order of the Many-Starred Cloak.** The Order of the Many-Starred Cloak is dedicated to defending and supporting Neverwinter, a city in the Forgotten Realms. In the past, the order has protected the city from rival mages in nearby Luskan, among other threats. Members include powerful mages such as the human Gallio Elibro, who's known for his planar research. The order might hire characters to investigate threats against the city, such as monsters amassed near Neverwinter or cults ensconced in the city.
- **Wizards Three.** The wizard Alustriel Silverhand called on her friends Tasha and Mordenkainen to thwart one of Vecna's plots. This alliance is known as the Wizards Three, and it is detailed in *Vecna: Eve of Ruin*. In that adventure, one of the Wizards Three isn't who they appear to be; you might continue this deception or decide the three wizards continue their alliance once Vecna is defeated.

#### Creating Your Own Wizards' Circle

Instead of using one of the wizards' circles described above, you can create a wizards' circle to serve as a group patron for your characters. You can use several of the archmage stat blocks in chapter 6, each of which specializes in one school of magic. If your world has a mages' guild, discussed earlier in this chapter, that guild might make an excellent wizards' circle group patron.

To make your own wizards' circle, start by creating one circle member for each of the player characters; this member acts as a personal patron to that character, while the circle as a whole supports the party's activities. For example, if one of your players has created a Sorcerer with the Draconic Sorcery subclass, the circle might include a dragon who has chosen the sorcerer as their personal agent. Other circle members, however, support different characters, and these members are the dragon's rivals.

When you create a wizards' circle for your campaign, you can roll on or choose from the Wizards' Circle Type table.

##### Wizards' Circle Type
| 1d6 | Patron Type                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
|:---:|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Council of the Wise. The circle members are the world's mightiest, most benevolent mages, but the most charismatic of them is secretly a traitor. Most of the characters' missions are altruistic, but occasionally the traitor sends them on missions meant to sow discord among the group.                            |
|  2  | Disaster Preventers. The circle members don't like each other, but they've come together to stop the apocalypse. They need the characters to achieve lesser missions while they focus on the larger threat.                                                                                                             |
|  3  | Grudging Allies. Longtime enemies, the circle members wreaked great destruction during their conflicts. They've agreed to avoid fighting each other, instead using the characters to pursue the goals they can agree on.                                                                                                |
|  4  | Pub Crew. The circle formed as an excuse to drink and socialize. Members have no time for dangerous missions to save the world, sending the characters instead.                                                                                                                                                         |
|  5  | War Veterans. The circle members are veterans who have served together in several wars. In their retirement, they prefer to send more energetic operatives—the characters—to stop the next great war.                                                                                                                   |
|  6  | Well-Meaning Imposters. The circle members are frauds with only minor magical power. They maintain this ruse because without the circle, the realm would collapse into warring factions. If the characters learn of the wizards' lack of ability, they might continue to work for the circle to avoid larger conflicts. |

#### Wizards' Circle Patron Perks

Characters in a party whose patron is a wizards' circle gain the following perks.

***Assisting Apprentices.*** The wizards in the circle have trained numerous apprentices, many of whom are loyal to their mentor. Whenever you're in a city, a former apprentice of a circle member can cast any level 1 or lower spell at no cost, as long as you identify yourself as an agent of that circle member. A former apprentice can cast any level 2–8 spell for you at half the usual cost, as long as you're in a city and similarly identify yourself.

***Compensation.*** The wizards' circle pays you to complete tasks on the circle's behalf. On average, the wizards' circle pays each member of your group 2 GP per day (enough to sustain a *Comfortable* lifestyle). Or you might be paid (at least 300 GP) after completing each task the wizards' circle assigns.

***Magical Resources.*** The wizards' circle has collected untold magic items and magical curiosities. This collection is scattered among the sanctums of the circle members. A wizards' circle can give members of the group any Common or Uncommon magic item in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*, as long as possession of the item would help the group's tasks. When the characters reach level 11, the wizards' circle can retrieve one Rare and one Very Rare magic item in between the group's tasks and award it to them as additional compensation. When the characters reach level 17, the wizards' circle can retrieve one Rare, Very Rare, or Legendary magic item in between tasks and award it to the group as additional compensation.

***Safe Houses.*** The wizards' circle maintains magically warded safe houses and hiding spots throughout the world or across multiple worlds. Your group knows how to locate these hidden redoubts and can sustain a *Modest* lifestyle in one for no cost. Revealing the location of one of these safe houses, whether purposefully or by accident, causes you to lose favor with the wizards' circle, and you might be banned from using them.

#### Wizards' Circle Contacts

The members of a wizards' circle don't always agree, and they keep secrets from each other. An individual in the circle might meet with only one or two characters at a time, in secret. In addition, the circle as a whole has an official spokesperson who communicates with the characters and gives them tasks.

Roll on or choose from the Wizards' Circle Contact table to determine who manages the relationship between the characters and the wizards' circle.

##### Wizards' Circle Contact
| 1d6 | Contact Type                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
|:---:|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
|  1  | Abjurer. Specializing in healing and protective magic, the contact wards the characters before adventures, heals them afterward, and urges the characters to be cautious.                                                  |
|  2  | Conjurer. The contact specializes in teleportation magic and summoning magical allies. If the party is in great danger, the contact teleports them to safety or sends magical creations to help.                           |
|  3  | Diviner. Before every mission, the contact delivers enigmatic advice that the characters find useful—if they can decipher its meaning and recall it at the right moment.                                                   |
|  4  | Evoker. This evoker proposes the most direct and destructive way to solve any problem. They don't worry about collateral damage incurred during a mission.                                                                 |
|  5  | Illusionist. The characters know little about the contact, not even their true appearance or identity. The contact could show up in any context or situation, even appearing to be someone else whom the party knows well. |
|  6  | Necromancer. The contact ghoulishly asks the characters to bring back corpses or body parts from their adventures but is willing to cast the *Raise Dead* spell if a character perishes on a mission.                      |

#### A Wizards' Circle Campaign

A campaign driven by a wizards' circle patron might unfold according to this outline:

- **Levels 1–4.** Each character in the group is selected by a single member of the wizards' circle and brought together to serve the circle. Early missions pit the group against opposing **Mage Apprentices**, **Nothics**, and other magical threats.
- **Levels 5–10.** When the characters seek to recover magical lore, obtain dangerous Artifacts, or slay magical foes, sometimes the object of their quest is missing or has already fled. It slowly becomes clear that someone inside the circle is feeding information to the circle's enemies.
- **Levels 11–16.** The traitorous member of the circle reveals themself and seizes control of the circle and its resources. Other members of the circle are imprisoned, slain, bought off, or cowed into working with the traitor. The characters might play along for a while or go on the run, but eventually they must confront the traitor and their allies.
- **Levels 17–20.** Now that the traitorous member of the circle is dealt with, the circle has openings for new members. The characters join the circle, recruiting new agents and rounding up the traitor's remaining followers.


------

# Chapter 4: Magical Factions

![Mages engage in a magical duel while members of the Crucible Keepers look on.](img/book/AU/043-04-001.ch-4-splash.webp)

Many scholars and practitioners of magic seek ever-greater prowess and status, but few can achieve these individually. Instead, those pursuing mystical knowledge often join influential groups. Numerous in membership and great in achievement, these factions accept adventurers into their folds, offering resources and knowledge in exchange for loyalty and service.

Each faction described in this chapter has its own outlook and goals, such as providing magical advice and entertainment to influence the powerful, researching alien forces from beyond the stars, and uncovering the secrets of life and undeath.

Faction members exchange magical discoveries, mentor pupils, and learn from more experienced colleagues. Members are united by shared specialties and philosophies. Although these organizations' disparate objectives often bring them into conflict with each other, members of different factions might also unite over common goals.

You can use these factions as patrons, as villains, or as organizations the characters can join. Adapt them to your world or campaign as needed.

## Faction Descriptions

This chapter includes details on the following factions:

- Bejeweled Conclave. The discreet enchanters of the Bejeweled Conclave make themselves invaluable to powerful clients as elite entertainers, advisers, and mediators while amassing clients' secrets.
- Bringers of Cosmic Dawn. Reclusive transmuters obsessed with mutations, the Bringers of Cosmic Dawn seek guidance from alien intelligences.
- Covenant of the Grave. The necromancers of the Covenant of the Grave study death and undeath with scientific precision, seeking esoteric knowledge through rigorous research.
- Crucible Keepers. From volcanic eruptions to shore-shredding hurricanes, no natural disaster is too dangerous for the daring Crucible Keepers to study in their pursuit of ever-escalating applications for Evocation magic.
- Horizon Weavers. The Horizon Weavers' conjurers practice summoning and teleportation as they explore and chart the multiverse.
- Ninth Quill. The spies and saboteurs of the Ninth Quill steal lore and artifacts from other powerful mages; many members do so to safeguard these treasures, but others are motivated by greed. The Ninth Quill also stokes dissent among the other factions to keep them from growing too powerful.
- Phantasmic Circus. The showy illusionists of the Phantasmic Circus entertain eager audiences with impossible spectacles.
- Seers of Sea and Sky. The diligent oracles of the Seers of Sea and Sky divine truths in the movements of the wind and waves.
- Sheltering Hands. The abjurers of the Sheltering Hands uplift the downtrodden and protect those in need.

## Magical Specializations

With the exception of the Ninth Quill, each of these magical factions specializes in a particular school of magic, as shown in the Factions by School table. Ninth Quill members instead dabble in all disciplines. These specializations are as much philosophical as technical. For instance, the fireball-throwing evokers of the Crucible Keepers are often forceful personalities who enjoy dramatic displays of destructive magic. In contrast, the subtle enchanters of the Bejeweled Conclave prefer to use smooth words to achieve their goals.

##### Factions by School
| School        | Faction                 |
|---------------|-------------------------|
| Abjuration    | Sheltering Hands        |
| Conjuration   | Horizon Weavers         |
| Divination    | Seers of Sea and Sky    |
| Enchantment   | Bejeweled Conclave      |
| Evocation     | Crucible Keepers        |
| Illusion      | Phantasmic Circus       |
| Necromancy    | Covenant of the Grave   |
| Transmutation | Bringers of Cosmic Dawn |

### Spellcasting Services

Magical factions include spellcasters who specialize in the associated school of magic. A faction's base of operations counts as a city for the purpose of purchasing spellcasting services (described in the *Player's Handbook*) for spells of the associated school. At your discretion, the casting of spells that belong to other schools might also be available for purchase, and faction members might offer discounts based on the characters' relationship with the faction.

### Renown

Characters can undertake missions and do favors for a faction and thereby earn renown with that faction without necessarily becoming members of the organization. Each faction description in this chapter includes benefits characters gain from accruing renown with the faction (as detailed in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*).

### Joining Factions

Although most members of magical factions are spellcasters, characters of any class can find a home in one or more of these factions. Each description includes the process for becoming a member of the faction; the benefits for doing so; and recommendations for classes, backgrounds, and proficiencies that make characters particularly well suited to the faction. The Factions by Class table provides a summary of which factions are a good fit for each class from the *Player's Handbook*.

#### Special Facilities

Each faction detailed in this chapter includes a special facility that members of that faction can add to their Bastions. The rules for Bastions appear in the *Dungeon Master's Guide*.

##### Factions by Class
| Class     | Suitable Factions                                             |
|-----------|---------------------------------------------------------------|
| Barbarian | Crucible Keepers                                              |
| Bard      | Bejeweled Conclave, Ninth Quill, Phantasmic Circus            |
| Cleric    | Covenant of the Grave, Seers of Sea and Sky, Sheltering Hands |
| Druid     | Crucible Keepers, Horizon Weavers, Seers of Sea and Sky       |
| Fighter   | Ninth Quill, Sheltering Hands                                 |
| Monk      | Covenant of the Grave, Sheltering Hands                       |
| Paladin   | Sheltering Hands                                              |
| Ranger    | Horizon Weavers, Seers of Sea and Sky                         |
| Rogue     | Bejeweled Conclave, Ninth Quill, Phantasmic Circus            |
| Sorcerer  | Bejeweled Conclave, Bringers of Cosmic Dawn, Crucible Keepers |
| Warlock   | Bejeweled Conclave, Bringers of Cosmic Dawn, Ninth Quill      |
| Wizard    | All                                                           |

![A Bejeweled Conclave diplomat magically calms a noble.](img/book/AU/044-04-002.bejeweled-conclave-diplomat.webp)

## Faction Relationships

The Faction Attitudes table summarizes the relationships between the factions described in this chapter. Faction pairs marked with an *F* consider each other allies; their members have a default attitude of Friendly toward one another. Pairs marked with an *H* consider each other rivals; their members have a default attitude of Hostile toward one another. Members of factions marked with an *I* begin as Indifferent toward one another. Exceptions might exist on an individual level.

##### Faction Attitudes
|                         | Bejeweled Conclave | Bringers of Cosmic Dawn | Covenant of the Grave | Crucible Keepers | Horizon Weavers | Ninth Quill | Phantasmic Circus | Seers of Sea and Sky | Sheltering Hands |
|-------------------------|:------------------:|:-----------------------:|:---------------------:|:----------------:|:---------------:|:-----------:|:-----------------:|:--------------------:|:----------------:|
| Bejeweled Conclave      |          F         |            I            |           H           |         H        |        F        |      H      |         F         |           I          |         I        |
| Bringers of Cosmic Dawn |          I         |            F            |           I           |         I        |        I        |      H      |         I         |           I          |         H        |
| Covenant of the Grave   |          H         |            I            |           F           |         I        |        I        |      H      |         H         |           I          |         I        |
| Crucible Keepers        |          H         |            I            |           I           |         F        |        I        |      H      |         I         |           F          |         I        |
| Horizon Weavers         |          F         |            I            |           I           |         I        |        F        |      H      |         I         |           I          |         F        |
| Ninth Quill             |          H         |            H            |           H           |         H        |        H        |      F      |         H         |           H          |         H        |
| Phantasmic Circus       |          F         |            I            |           H           |         I        |        I        |      H      |         F         |           I          |         I        |
| Seers of Sea and Sky    |          I         |            I            |           I           |         F        |        I        |      H      |         I         |           F          |         I        |
| Sheltering Hands        |          I         |            H            |           I           |         I        |        F        |      H      |         I         |           I          |         F        |

## Bejeweled Conclave

The Bejeweled Conclave is known as an organization of professional entertainers and mediators who cater to wealthy clients. They host glamorous parties, serve as courtiers, broker deals, and provide entertainment for exclusive functions.

But the Bejeweled Conclave wields far more influence than its publicly known services indicate. Consummate agents behind the throne, the enchanters of the Bejeweled Conclave cultivate elite connections and shift the balance of power in cities across the world.

### Goals and Activities

![This unassuming baker is, in reality, a member of the Bejeweled Conclave's Whisper Network.](img/book/AU/045-04-003.bejeweled-conclave-baker.webp)

The Bejeweled Conclave seeks to increase its sway across the multiverse. The faction engages skilled diplomats, courtiers, and entertainers and hones their abilities. Enchanters and spies receive a more clandestine, but no less thorough, education. The conclave prizes subtlety and secrecy above all; members are taught that it's better to fail a mission and keep the conclave's secrets than to succeed at the cost of exposing the faction's true goals.

The Bejeweled Conclave operates in major cities throughout the multiverse. Distance keeps members from knowing all their peers, but the faction is quick to celebrate successes, building loyalty and camaraderie within its ranks. While all members learn conversational tricks to offer convincing praise, the compliments members bestow on each other are usually genuine.

Conclave members understand that overtly using Enchantment magic risks exposing them as more than entertainers and courtiers. If a member must modify a noble's memories or magically dominate a general, they undertake significant planning to conceal their involvement.

### Leadership and Organization

More experienced members of the Bejeweled Conclave oversee operations assigned to junior members. Valuable secrets are reported through this hierarchy up to the faction's highest ranks, referred to as the Whisper Network. These leaders hoard a treasure trove of secrets; there are few facts about the movers and shakers of civilization that the Bejeweled Conclave doesn't know.

Conclave members are trusted to act alone when necessary, such as when an important client insists on hiring only a single mediator or companion. When possible, though, senior members politely request that their "junior clerks" or "associates in training" handle more workaday aspects of their affairs. Such requests, which might include subtle magical compulsion, are a ruse to bring another trained member to operate from the shadows. As a result, clients who planned to planned to hire only one member of the Bejeweled Conclave often find themselves engaging two or three.

#### Whisper Network

Members of the Whisper Network keep their identities private, even from their peers. The only exception is Madame Hestia Ilverne (Medium **Noble Prodigy**), a courteous human Whisper Network member who heads the conclave's public-facing efforts. Rumors within the faction claim that Madame Ilverne is among the lowest ranked of the leaders, but she seems nothing but pleased with her position.

Whether members of the Whisper Network work as music instructors, clerks, dancers, or cooks, they keep an eye on all aspects of Bejeweled Conclave activities. If they have edicts or missions to impart, they do so through Madame Ilverne. When she casually mentions a task to a member with the words "The Network would prefer a favor," all comprehend the seriousness of the request.

#### Lurewood Mansion
